Self-as-an-End Theory Series · SAE War Treatise (v2)

SAE War Treatise: How Cultivation Is Not Swallowed by War Itself
SAE战争论：涵育如何不被战争本身吞噬

Han Qin (秦汉)  ·  Independent Researcher  ·  2026
DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.19656617  ·  Full PDF on Zenodo  ·  CC BY 4.0
Abstract

This treatise is not a philosophy of war. It asks how cultivation (涵育), when it operates in the situation of war, is not swallowed by war itself. Cultivation — the continuous deepening, unfolding, and growth of subjectivity through time — is the true home-ground of the Kingdom of Ends. War's position in this framework is that of an extreme state cultivation must address. The derivation: cultivation is the goal; war is a last-resort means. The only legitimate reason for war's existence is that through it, cultivation obtains the conditions to continue. The treatise derives the SAE criteria for qualified war, the structural distinctions between the Kingdom of Ends and the Kingdom of Means in conflict, and the conditions under which war-logic swallows the subjects who enter it.

Keywords: SAE, war, cultivation, Kingdom of Ends, Kingdom of Means, qualified war, subjectivity, 14DD

Writing statement: This treatise was written in collaboration with several AI collaborators (Claude, ChatGPT, Gemini, Grok). All intellectual decisions, framework design, and final editorial judgments were made by the author, who bears full responsibility for the text.

Prologue

1. Why This Has to Be Written

Writing this is not something I particularly wanted to do.

Over these years the SAE framework has advanced from life to consciousness, from economics to law, from education to methodology, and every paper has been oriented toward cultivation, toward the release of subjectivity, toward the positive construction of the Kingdom of Ends. War is not in this orientation. War is what the Kingdom of Ends must address but least wishes to occur. Writing a treatise on war, even only to complete the theoretical architecture, is like setting aside a corner of a clean room expressly to keep a scar. I do it unwillingly.

But not writing is not an option.

Given where SAE now stands, silence on war is itself a stance — and precisely the kind of stance SAE's own methodology would critique. Silence means surrendering the field of war to other discourses to go on dominating it, tacitly conceding that the war logic of the Kingdom of Means holds final interpretive authority over this terrain. However good the reasons for silence, the cost of silence is leaving the Kingdom of Ends without usable criteria when it comes to war. When the next war arrives — whether some conflict on Azeroth or a confrontation in any other field — someone in the middle of it who wants to use "persons as ends" as a criterion for action will find that SAE has given him no ready language.

This treatise is for that person.

Not for those who start wars, nor for those who look on at them. For the one who cannot not enter war, yet still hopes not to slide out of the Kingdom of Ends.

The Core of This Treatise: Cultivation

Before entering the theory proper, the true core of this treatise must be established.

This treatise is not "a philosophy of war."

This treatise is "how cultivation, in the situation of war, is not swallowed by war itself."

Cultivation is the principled foundation of the entire SAE framework. What is cultivation? It is the process of subjectivity continuously deepening, unfolding, and growing in one another through time. It is not a static state but an ongoing growth. It does not belong to any particular subject; it belongs to the structure that all subjects together continuously enact. The cultivation of a civilization, the cultivation of a society, the cultivation of an individual life, the cultivation of an inter-subjective relationship — each is an ongoing process of growth.

Cultivation is the true home-ground of the Kingdom of Ends. Development, mutual-chiseling (互凿), the deepening of subjectivity, the continuous growth of a civilization as a whole — these are concrete forms of cultivation. All the positive work of the Kingdom of Ends is the unfolding of cultivation.

War's position in this framework is — an extreme state that cultivation must address. When cultivation is interrupted by the expansion/oppression of 14DD systemic purpose, when subjectivity can no longer grow under that oppression, when fa-mou (伐谋, attacking through strategy) and fa-jiao (伐交, attacking through diplomacy) are exhausted, war serves as the backstop that gives cultivation a chance to recover.

War is a means; cultivation is the goal. The only legitimate reason for war's existence as a means is — through it, cultivation obtains the conditions to continue.

This makes the derivation chain of the treatise clear:

  • The four theorems do not establish "norms of war"; they establish the protective mechanism by which cultivation, in war, is not swallowed by war itself
  • The three ending forms do not classify "war's results"; they analyze how cultivation recovers after war ends
  • Yielding to the upward sequence does not mean "from war back to peace"; it means "letting cultivation return to its proper home-ground position"

Cultivation runs through the entire treatise. Every criterion, every theorem, every structural observation serves cultivation — letting cultivation go on even in the situation of war.

Isomorphism with SAE Legal Philosophy

This treatise's position in the SAE framework is completely isomorphic with SAE Legal Philosophy.

Law: a means, whose goal is to make cultivation possible. Through handling ordinary collisions (14DD against 14DD at the individual level), it lets cultivation among subjects continue without being interrupted by collision.

War: a means, whose goal is to make cultivation possible. Through handling extreme-state collisions (14DD systemic purpose's expansion/oppression against 15DD), it allows cultivation to recover even when it has been interrupted.

Both are protective mechanisms of cultivation, not cultivation itself. Cultivation is the true home-ground of the Kingdom of Ends. Law and war are both protective tools that cannot not be brought into use when the home-ground meets obstacles.

This isomorphism matters because it tells the reader at once — this treatise's overall posture is consistent with SAE Legal Philosophy's overall posture. Legal Philosophy is not a study of "how law becomes perfect"; it is a study of "how cultivation is protected through law in situations of collision." The War Treatise is not a study of "how war becomes qualified"; it is a study of "how cultivation is protected through war in the situation of war."

Law handles ordinary collisions; war handles extreme-state collisions. In the protection of cultivation the two are complementary; in principle they are isomorphic.

Clarifying the Ground of Derivation

Before entering the theory proper, one matter of ground that is very easily misread must be clarified.

All the derivations of SAE war theory — including "war protects cultivation," "do not lose, not win," "the four theorems," and so on — do not start from "protecting cultivation is just." SAE has no concept of "justice." Nor does SAE have "ethics" as an independent concept (One's Own Law: The SAE Critique of Ethics and Morality (DOI 10.5281/zenodo.19037566) deals with how the discourse of "ethics" operates at the 15DD layer; it does not set up a new body of ethical norms). The reader should not read any statement in this treatise as "what SAE holds to be a just war."

What, then, is the ground of derivation for SAE war theory?

It is SAE's axiom itself — subjectivity cannot be eliminated, cultivation is the form in which subjectivity grows, cultivation cannot not continue.

This is a structural statement, not a value statement. Cultivation cannot not continue, not because it "ought to continue," but because structurally it simply is continuing. Acts that oppress cultivation fail structurally — oppressed subjectivity returns in many forms: resistance, splitting, self-reconstruction, re-emergence in the next generation, persistence as traumatic memory among the descendants of the oppressed that is eventually transformed into action. This is not a moral commandment; it is a structural observation.

So the derivation chain of war theory runs like this:

Not — "cultivation ought to be protected, so war that protects cultivation is just."

But — "cultivation, as the growth of subjectivity, is a structural fact; acts that oppress cultivation necessarily trigger a returning force; what war theory has to do is let this returning force run under qualified structural constraints, so that it does not itself interrupt cultivation as well."

The two derivations stand on entirely different ground. The first hangs SAE on a moral criterion — and any moral criterion can be challenged by another moral criterion, so the first ground is fluid. The second hangs SAE on SAE's own axiom — "subjectivity cannot be eliminated, cultivation cannot not continue," as an axiom, needs no external moral justification; it is the starting point of the SAE framework. The second ground is stable.

This clarification is not wordplay. It determines how every statement in this treatise is to be read. When the text below says "a certain war is qualified under SAE," it does not mean "this war is morally right"; it means "this war has not structurally interrupted cultivation." When the text says "a certain war is unqualified under SAE," it does not mean "this war is morally wrong"; it means "this war is already structurally interrupting cultivation." Qualified and unqualified are structural judgments, not moral judgments.

This clarification of the ground has a deeper consequence as well — SAE does not judge history. All the wars of history, all the military strategists, all the thinkers of war made the judgments they could make in their own eras and from their own structural positions. The work Sun Tzu did at the top of 14DD two thousand three hundred years ago is a real achievement of human wisdom; he had not read Kant, and the language of "persons as ends" was not available to him. This treatise does not mean to say that he was wrong; it means to say that he stopped where he could reach, and that the work from there on needs a new language to be completed. This is the stance of cultivation, not the stance of judgment.

Likewise, this treatise does not hand the reader a yardstick for judging others. The criteria it establishes are first of all for subjects to use on themselves — so that a subject who cannot not enter war has, while waging it, a set of criteria by which to examine whether its own actions are still in the direction of cultivation. Turned inward, the criterion is an instrument of reflection; turned outward (to judge others), it slides into an instrument of 14DD systemic purpose. SAE's criteria are the former, not the latter.

The ground is now clarified. The main text follows.

2. The Position of This Treatise

This treatise's position within the SAE architecture must be stated first, so that the reader does not take it for the opening of a series.

It is not a series. It is a single paper. In position it is isomorphic with the SAE Critique of Ethics and Morality and SAE Legal Philosophy — all belong to the supplementary layer. "Supplementary layer" means that the main structure of the SAE theoretical edifice (cultivation, subjectivity, the Kingdom of Ends) has already been built, but certain structural gaps must be filled, or the edifice cannot operate in the corresponding fields. War is one such gap. Law is another. The two stand in isomorphic positions — both are protective mechanisms of cultivation, both handle collisions; only the form of collision they handle differs. Law handles ordinary collisions (14DD against 14DD at the individual level); war handles extreme-state collisions (14DD systemic purpose's expansion/oppression against 15DD). Neither is cultivation itself; both serve cultivation.

This treatise fills the gap of war, and then steps aside.

Stepping aside means — once written, the War Treatise does not expand, does not upgrade into a series, does not become a new focal direction of research. After it is written, author and reader both return their attention to cultivation itself — to development, to ethics, to the positive construction of subjectivity. The War Treatise is a single handling, not a new home-ground. Cultivation is the home-ground.

This posture matters. For if, in the course of being written, the War Treatise turned into a shift of SAE's center of gravity toward the study of war, that would show that SAE itself had already failed to hold methodologically — letting the work of the supplementary layer usurp the host's place, letting the means (war) override the end (cultivation). The single-paper form of this treatise is a self-constraint: once the handling is done, it yields to cultivation.

3. Three Boundaries and One Discipline

To keep this treatise in the place where it belongs, several boundaries are drawn in advance.

First, this treatise does not touch specific war events on Earth, nor specific military technologies on Earth. As philosophical work, war theory gives direction, not quantitative prescription — specific tactics, equipment, force organization, logistics, and the like all belong to the working domain of military and political leaders, and philosophy has no standing to speak at these levels. All the concrete examples in this treatise come from Azeroth — an independent field of thought experiment. Generic historical categories (labels such as "colonial wars" or "wars of liberation") are not covered by this restriction.

Second, this treatise does not follow the evolution of real-world forms of war. Whatever technological form future wars take (drones, algorithms, space), the four theorems of SAE war theory do not change. Principle does not follow phenomena; principle judges phenomena.

The third is the most important boundary — philosophy's lightness and the executor's weight.

This treatise establishes four theorems, three ending forms, and the Methods-Constraint Bridge — all of these are directional principles. But how the protection of cultivation is concretely realized in the moment, those decision points, balance points, threshold judgments, choices of means — when fa-mou and fa-jiao count as exhausted, when cultivation counts as pushed to the critical threshold, when expansion is necessary, when a ceasefire is due, whether a specific means in a specific situation has sealed off the other side's possibility of reorganization — these judgments are not ones this treatise is able to give.

These judgments belong to the working domain of military and political leaders.

Acknowledging this boundary is a necessary condition of this treatise's honesty. A philosophical paper is written in a study; it does not make decisions at the front. A philosophical paper does not face particular soldiers, does not face death in the present moment, does not face judgments that must be made within a few hours and that bear on the fates of thousands or tens of thousands of people. A philosophical paper can spend months polishing a sentence; military and political leaders often have only minutes to decide a matter.

The weight that military and political leaders carry in making those judgments is a weight philosophers cannot bear. What they bear is the full weight of concrete decisions — whether to start the war at this particular moment, whether to use this particular means, whether to cease fire at this particular time. Behind every judgment are real people, real consequences, real historical responsibility. What the philosopher can do is, from far away, in a very quiet study, think the directional principles through clearly, and then hand them over.

This treatise's task is far simpler, and far lighter, than the executors' task. What this treatise can give is — the direction "war as a means serves cultivation," the constraining principles of the four theorems, and, under these principles, what counts as structurally qualified and what counts as structural slippage. How to judge in a specific situation, how to strike the balance, how to choose under pressure — this treatise cannot give it, and should not pretend that it can.

This is not modesty; it is clear-headedness. If philosophy crossed this boundary to give quantitative guidance, it would both do the job badly (because it lacks the information of the situation the executors are in) and be disrespectful to the executors (because it pretends it can bear their weight for them). SAE war theory acknowledges this boundary explicitly, actively, without concealment — Direction is given by philosophy; decisions are made by the executors; responsibility is borne by the executors.

The fourth is not a boundary but a discipline — the discipline of cultivation.

This treatise does not judge history. 14DD collision is the real structure of the encounter between subjects, the unavoidable mutual contact that occurs as cultivation unfolds among subjects — cultivation itself needs collision (mutual-chiseling is always needed), and subjects without collision wither. The work that military strategists and thinkers of war have done at the top of 14DD over two thousand three hundred years is a real achievement of human wisdom — in their own positions, so that the cultivation of their own times would not be wholly swallowed by war, they did the work they could do. Sun Tzu's insight at the top of 14DD was right; it is only that he had not read Kant, and that the languages of "persons as ends" and of "cultivation as the home-ground of the Kingdom of Ends" were not available to him. In the position he could reach, he went as far as it is possible to go. This treatise does not aim to negate Sun Tzu; it aims to take up his work and push it to where he did not push it — onto cultivation as the principled foundation.

Likewise, every person in every war of history made the choices they could make in the position they occupied. Often they did not know what they were doing — they thought they were defending their homes, defending their honor, defending justice, when in fact they were defending one concrete form of cultivation. They did not have the word "cultivation," but what they structurally served was cultivation. SAE does not judge them. SAE only gives those who come later one more language, so that those who come later can see more clearly what they themselves are doing in similar situations.

4. The Dimensional Positioning of This Treatise

What layer of problem does war theory deal with? This section declares it in advance, so that the reader does not enter the treatise with the wrong expectations.

War, as the phenomenon of violent confrontation, is started by 14DD systemic purpose and falls at the 14DD layer — the extreme state of collision between subjects. A conflict between two or more subjects has come to the point of violent confrontation. This object itself belongs to the 14DD domain.

But this treatise's handling is at the 15DD layer. Why? Because the core mechanism by which war can happen, can persist, can expand, can violate principle lies at 15DD — in whether the war has already been swallowed by 14DD systemic purpose (state ideology, national narrative, justice-discourse, war ethics) or is still protecting cultivation. When a war is being fought, the specific tactics, equipment, and strategy are matters of the 14DD layer; but whether this war is still serving cultivation, whether it has already slid into a Kingdom-of-Means war that interrupts cultivation — that judgment is made at the 15DD layer.

So this treatise is not a book on the art of war. The work on the art of war at the top of 14DD has been carried very deep by Sun Tzu and others. This treatise is at the 15DD layer — the layer of principle above the art of war and below Kant's second formulation. A reader who expects this treatise to tell him how to fight a war will be disappointed; a reader who expects it to tell him what kind of war, under SAE criteria, is a qualified war serving cultivation — that is the question this treatise sets out to answer.

This treatise's criterion, in one sentence — when does war as a means still serve cultivation, and when has it become a tool that interrupts cultivation? The four theorems are the concretization of this criterion.

Upper Volume · Locating

The Upper Volume locates war — within the overall SAE architecture, what war is, where it is, and what relations it has with the structures around it. Once this locating work is complete, the Middle Volume can proceed to the four theorems that constrain war, and the Lower Volume to the three ending forms. Without locating, the criteria lose their footing.

The eight sections of the Upper Volume each take one angle of locating, and together they give war a complete structural position:

§5 establishes war's origin structure: the expansion/oppression of 14DD systemic purpose triggers 15DD's defensive resistance; it also establishes the five-level upward sequence (上兵伐谋 shang-bing fa-mou → 超兵伐辩 chao-bing fa-bian → 极兵伐思 ji-bing fa-si → 玄兵不伐 xuan-bing bu-fa → 圣者不兵 sheng-zhe bu-bing) as the spectrum of collision handling outside war. War is the backstop extreme state after all five levels have failed.

§6 establishes war's isomorphic position with law: both are protective mechanisms of cultivation. Law handles ordinary collisions; war handles extreme-state collisions. Neither is cultivation itself; both serve cultivation.

§7 establishes war's position as a means: war is a means whose goal is to make cultivation possible. War is not an end, and it can never be elevated into an end.

§8 establishes war's objective function: do not lose, not win. True losing is the war-party interrupting its own civilization's cultivation in the course of the war, not military victory or defeat.

§9 establishes war's direction: liberating subjectivity — letting subjectivity oppressed by 14DD systemic purpose regain the space for cultivation.

§10 establishes the work of cultivation outside war: not fighting is also preparing for war — deepening cultivation itself in peacetime is the best preparation for war, because a civilization with deeper cultivation is stronger both in speed of development and in overall capacity.

§11 establishes war's relation to development: the form cultivation takes under non-oppressive conditions is called development. War is the extreme response when cultivation is interrupted; development is the unfolding of cultivation under normal conditions. SAE does not promise historical necessity — it is not certain that barbarism cannot conquer civilization — but over the long run, subjects with deeper cultivation prevail with high probability.

§12 establishes the technical branching within war: mou (strategy, legitimate) · yin-mou (scheming, mediocre) · yang-mou (open strategy, truly effective) · gui (deception, illegitimate — the crux being the oppression of one's own side's subjectivity). A four-level spectrum. The four-quadrant analysis of gui unfolds across four positions: 14DD systemic purpose against 14DD systemic purpose, 15DD against 15DD, the 15DD party, and the 14DD party.

Together the eight sections give war its complete position within SAE — where it comes from (14DD expansion), what it stands parallel to (law), what it is (a means), what it is to do (let cultivation recover), what its alternative is (the deepening of cultivation itself), how it relates to development (interrupted vs. normal unfolding), and how it branches internally (mou/yin-mou/yang-mou/gui).

The completion of the Upper Volume lets the reader see one thing — war as a means occupies a position lower than the cultivation it is meant to protect. War is not the high point of the Kingdom of Ends; it is the lowest backstop the Kingdom of Ends cannot not activate when threatened. Every section of the Upper Volume reinforces this position from a different angle — and war's low position gives the Middle Volume's four constraint theorems (Cannot-Not-Starting, Cannot-Not-Expanding, Cannot-Not-Be-Directed-Toward-Ending, Cannot-Not-Be-Open-to-Questioning) their footing. Precisely because war's position is low, its activation, its continuation, its direction, and the state of the subjects who wage it must all be strictly constrained — so that a tool whose position is low to begin with does not turn around and take hostage the higher thing it is meant to protect.

The eight sections follow.

5. The Object of This Treatise and the Complete Sequence of Collision Handling

This treatise's object is war. What is war? Before proceeding, the origin structure of war must be made clear — the beginning of war is always the 15DD party defending against the 14DD party's attack.

Every part of this sentence carries a structural function and needs to be unfolded.

The Essence of 14DD Systemic Purpose: Expansion

The internal operating mode of 14DD systemic purpose is expansion.

14DD systemic purpose is a target construction external to concrete subjects — national interest, ideological realization, the enforcement of a national narrative, the promotion of a systematized justice-discourse, the propagation of some "thus it ought to be" form of civilization. Such targets are never completed within current boundaries — they always require "more": more resources, more territory, more compliers, more executive tools, more space for ideological diffusion. Thus 14DD systemic purpose necessarily points toward expansion.

This is not some particular 14DD's "badness" — it is the internal logic of 14DD systemic purpose as a structural type. A 14DD system cannot sustain its systemic purpose without expanding. For its purpose, structurally speaking, is "to make some systematized 'ought' cover more of reality."

A terminological limitation must be established here — what this treatise calls "14DD expansion" refers specifically to 14DD operation after it has been externalized as systemic purpose. It does not mean every individual 14DD is inherently expansionist. The individual-level 14DD discussed in other SAE papers (the Learning Series, the Developmental Psychology Series, etc.) is the subject's own "cannot-not," a necessary structure for the unfolding of cultivation — not inherently evil, not inherently expansionist, not inherently instrumentalizing of others. What expands, instrumentalizes, and starts wars is 14DD systemic purpose — the kind of 14DD that is externalized, systematized, and demands compliance from others. Every statement in this treatise about "14DD expansion/oppression" must be read under this limitation and not extended to individual 14DD.

When expansion stalls, 14DD systemic purpose does not truly stop either — it goes dormant.

When 14DD temporarily cannot expand (external powers too strong, internal crisis, insufficient resources, lack of opportunity), it does not abandon its expansion target and turn toward cultivation — it goes dormant, waiting for the conditions of expansion to reappear. During dormancy, 14DD still maintains the structural preparation for expansion: ideological mobilization, military buildup, internal oppression (readying its own people to be mobilized at any moment), continuous external pressure. Dormancy is expansion paused, not the disappearance of expansionist intent.

This makes 14DD's "peacetime" completely different from genuine 15DD cultivation — 14DD's "peace" is expansion paused, not the disappearance of expansionist intent. 15DD has no need to "pause" expansion, because it does not have expansion in the first place.

14DD's External Expansion and Internal Oppression Are One

For 14DD to expand, it must instrumentalize the subjects within it — subjects must become execution units of the expansion machine and cannot exist as complete subjects (complete subjects may refuse mobilization, may question the rationale for expansion, may demand that their subjectivity be recognized). Thus 14DD's systemic operation necessarily oppresses internally.

14DD's internal oppression and external expansion are two sides of the same structure — unless a 14DD is completely isolated (nearly impossible in the modern world), the two occur together. A 14DD that systematically oppresses its own people also expands outward; a 14DD that is expanding outward is also oppressing internally.

This makes the "preparation period" and the "war period" structurally continuous — 14DD's systematic internal oppression is itself preparation for attack. The external 15DD party, seeing 14DD's internal oppression intensify, knows this to be a potential attack directed at itself and must prepare to defend.

15DD's "Purpose": All 15DD Are the Same

All subjects genuinely operating at 15DD share one "purpose" — recognizing subjects as ends in themselves (Kant's second formulation).

This is not any particular 15DD subject's "own purpose" — it is the structural orientation shared by all 15DD subjects. The content of the 15DD "purpose" is the same across different 15DD subjects: letting every subject exist as an end.

This means 15DD has no "systemic purpose" to speak of — because the very concept of "systemic purpose" is a 14DD construction. The 15DD "purpose" is for subjects to be subjects, and this does not need to be systematized; it is simply the authentic operation of the subject.

Because the content of every 15DD "purpose" is identical, there is no need for war between 15DD subjects — they do not come to blows over a "conflict of purposes." Two 15DD subjects may differ in their concrete paths of realization (recognizing subjects through way of life A vs. through way of life B), but their underlying "purpose" is the same.

This also means 15DD does not actively oppress others. For the 15DD "purpose" (letting all subjects be ends) does not, structurally, require oppression to be realized — on the contrary, oppressing others directly violates the 15DD "purpose" (the one oppressed is also a subject). Thus 15DD has no necessity of expansion; it carries out cultivation where it stands and does not need to maintain itself through expansion.

The True Structure of War: 14DD Attacks, 15DD Defends and Resists

The two layers above combined — 14DD necessarily expands (or goes dormant preparing to expand again), and 15DD does not actively oppress — make the true structure of war clear.

The beginning of war is always the 14DD party's attack; the 15DD party's resistance is defense, not attack.

The 14DD party's attack has two forms —

Form one: active external attack. The 14DD party directly invades, by military force, the territory, system, culture, or subject-position of the 15DD party. Or, outside wartime, it carries out structural erosion of the 15DD party through economic pressure, diplomatic coercion, ideological penetration, and the like.

Form two: internal oppression of its own subjects as preparation for attack. The 14DD party systematically oppresses its own people — instrumentalizing them, building mobilization machinery, systematically canceling their subjectivity. This internal oppression is itself preparation for attack — because for a 14DD capable of systematically oppressing its own people, outward expansion is only a matter of time. The external 15DD party, seeing this internal oppression intensify, knows it to be a potential attack directed at itself and must prepare to defend.

The two forms are structurally one thing — both are the outward radiation of 14DD's systematic expansion/oppression. Whether responding to an invasion that has already arrived or to an attack being prepared, the 15DD party stands in the position of "defense."

The Object of This Treatise

The structure established above determines the range of this treatise's object —

This treatise handles: the 14DD party, with 14DD systemic purpose as its reason, expanding externally or oppressing internally as preparation for expansion; and the 15DD party, which must resist in order to defend subjectivity. This is the complete object of SAE war theory.

14DD against 14DD is not within this treatise's discussion range. Collisions between two 14DD systemic purposes are conflicts between two directions of expansion (two empires contending for hegemony, two ideological systems in opposition, two sets of state interests overlapping at a border). Such wars have recurred throughout human history, but neither their motivational structure nor the ways of responding to them is the object of SAE war theory. SAE war theory does not establish criteria for such collisions — because both sides operate at 14DD, neither uses "subjects as ends" as the starting point for judgment, and SAE criteria do not function for them. These matters between 14DDs are handled by 14DD's own order (diplomacy, treaties, balance of power, international law, etc.); they are not this treatise's work.

15DD against 15DD is not within this treatise's discussion range either. Two subjects operating at 15DD may collide, but the collision need not escalate to war. Collision is handled through discourse — 超兵伐辩 (chao-bing fa-bian, the transcending bing attacks by argumentative-exchange; on bing, see the five-level sequence below). Discourse is not the opposition of two ideologies (that is 14DD systemic purpose against 14DD systemic purpose); it is an inquiry, between two subjects already at one on "recognizing subjects," into how that recognition can happen better in different concrete forms. Discourse can be intense and deep, but it does not need violence. For there is no "conflict of purposes" between the two sides, only "differences in paths of realization."

This treatise handles only — the 14DD party's expansion or preparation for expansion, and the 15DD party's defensive resistance.

Two more forms of collision lie outside this treatise's range, and they must be laid down separately to complete the definition of the object.

13DD against 13DD — ecological-niche contestation. Collisions between two populations that have not yet formed systematized cultural/ideological structures. In the SAE DD sequence, 13DD is the primordial state in which the subject has not yet stably formed — at the individual level corresponding to instinctual drives, survival needs, emotional impulses, and other pre-reflective layers; at the civilizational level corresponding to tribal/clan collective life that has not yet formed a systematized culture.

Collisions between two 13DD populations are not what SAE defines as war — there is no expansion of systemic purpose (because a 14DD system has not yet formed), and no defense of subjectivity (because 15DD subjects have not yet stably formed). What the collision is about is the contest for an ecological niche — fighting over resources, territory, food, room to reproduce.

Such collisions are at most small-scale conflicts, not wars in this treatise's sense. Collisions of this kind occurred again and again in human prehistory (some contacts between different early human populations belong to this category). They are not within this treatise's handling range.

13DD against 14DD — contestation for cultural dominance. Collisions between a civilization with systematized culture and a population that has not yet formed a civilization. Civilizational expansion draws non-civilizational populations into its own cultural sphere.

Such collisions are not what SAE defines as war either — because the 13DD side has no structure of 15DD defense (it has no stable subjectivity yet to defend); at most it passively undergoes absorption by the culturally dominant side. What 14DD civilizational expansion meets in this situation is not the resistance of subjects but unorganized individual reactions.

Many such contacts have later been narrated as "conquest," but structurally, in SAE terms, they are not wars between two systems — they are absorption by the culturally dominant side, 14DD systemic purpose absorbing 13DD populations. These too are not within this treatise's handling range.

So SAE-defined war has only one form — 14DD systemic purpose's expansion/oppression triggering 15DD's defensive resistance. All other forms of collision (14DD systemic purpose against 14DD systemic purpose, 15DD against 15DD, 13DD against 13DD, 13DD against 14DD) are not war in this treatise's sense, and none is within this treatise's handling range. With this, the definition of this treatise's object is complete.

The Deepest Closure: Seen from Cultivation's Perspective

The definition of the object above is horizontal — it excludes one object after another. But seen from a different angle, each exclusion has its own principled reason — seen from cultivation's perspective — and this treatise's range contracts to an even harder sentence.

14DD systemic purpose against 14DD systemic purpose — non-cultivation mutual-chiseling. A collision between two systemic purposes has no cultivation operating in the field; it is merely expansion against expansion, tool against tool. On neither side of the collision is cultivation present as home-ground. Such collisions we do not want to discuss — because cultivation is not in the field, and this treatise has no motive to establish criteria here.

15DD against 15DD — cultivation mutual-chiseling. A collision between two subjects genuinely operating at 15DD is the deepest form of cultivation unfolding between the two sides. Discourse and walking-away are themselves the direct work of cultivation — subjects mutually revealing, mutually deepening, mutually honing. Such collisions we do not need to discuss — because cultivation is unfolding and is being protected by both sides, and does not need this treatise to establish criteria to protect it.

13DD against 13DD, 13DD against 14DD — cultivation has not yet stably formed in the field (because 15DD subjects have not yet stably formed). This treatise's criteria do not apply in these situations, because what the criteria protect (cultivation) is not yet there.

So the only situation this treatise cannot not discuss is — 14DD wanting to oppress 15DD.

Only in this situation is it the case that cultivation is already in the field (the 15DD party is a genuine subject, and cultivation is unfolding on the 15DD side), that cultivation faces the threat of being swallowed completely (the expansion of 14DD systemic purpose seeks to degrade the 15DD party into a tool), and that cultivation cannot protect itself (the authentic operation of 15DD contains no tool of "oppression"; it can only defend and resist).

This is this treatise's only cannot-not — the protection of cultivation in this one situation.

This contracts the treatise's object further, beyond "war" — war itself is not this treatise's object; cultivation's not being swallowed in one specific situation is. The War Treatise is only one concrete manifestation of the work of protecting cultivation — in this specific situation, where cultivation is threatened by the expansion of systemic purpose, how cultivation can recover through the activation of war as an extreme-state tool. War is the means, not the object; cultivation is this treatise's true concern.

The Structural Challenge in 15DD's Resistance

15DD's resistance is defense, but resistance itself brings a structural challenge — resistance requires organization, mobilization, wartime discipline, centralized decision-making. These requirements are structurally similar to 14DD's systematized mode of operation. In the course of resisting, the 15DD party runs the risk of sliding toward 14DD through the organizational needs of its own resistance — if resisters are not vigilant, they may become another 14DD in the process of resisting 14DD.

This is why the four theorems, the three ending forms, and the anti-yu-sui (anti-honorable-annihilation) criterion set out later all need to be strict — they constrain not only the "oppressing party" but, still more, the resisting party itself from sliding toward the structure it resists. 15DD's victory is not "defeating 14DD and establishing our own 14DD system"; it is letting the resistance truly end at the moment when "cultivation can continue again," and not letting the organizational machinery of resistance persist after the war and develop into a new 14DD system.

This challenge will be concretely unfolded in both §18 (15DD resistance succeeds) and §19 (15DD resistance fails).

The Complete Sequence of Collision Handling

SAE has a complete upward sequence for handling collisions between subjects. This sequence is not five levels of war — war is not within this sequence; war is the backstop extreme state after all five levels have failed. The reader should carry this prior understanding into what follows.

Within the overall SAE framework, this sequence is isomorphic with the Via Rho dual path of Methodology 00 (Via Rho: The Way of the Remainder) and with the fractal hierarchical structure of Method VI v2 — collision handling unfolding at different levels with increasing purity, each level being the natural form of cultivation at that depth of collision.

In this sequence, bing names a category of action, not military personnel. The five levels are unfolded below; each is not "some kind of bing" but a way in which collision is handled:

上兵伐谋 Shang-bing fa-mou — the highest bing attacks by strategy (14DD top). Sun Tzu's position. Inter-subject collision is handled at the level of mou; what is attacked is the other's strategy, the other's plans, the other's dispositions. Both sides still recognize each other as subjects; bing exists as a contest at the level of wisdom.

超兵伐辩 Chao-bing fa-bian — the transcending bing attacks by argumentative-exchange (15DD against 15DD). Two 15DD subjects interrogate each other, shake each other, let each other be revealed. To attack here is not to defeat; it is to let revelation happen. The endpoint of discourse is each subject revealing itself more clearly.

极兵伐思 Ji-bing fa-si — the ultimate bing attacks by self-thought (before the collision takes form). The point at which the subject of the sequence shifts. What fa-si attacks is not the other's thought but one's own thought — "why have I not yet developed to the point where this collision becomes meaningless to the other?" The focus of the question moves from the other side back to oneself. The other side turns from an object-of-handling into a mirror-for-reflection.

玄兵不伐，走为先 Xuan-bing bu-fa, zou wei xian — the mysterious bing does not attack; walking comes first (recognizing the field). Here the act of attacking is canceled. "Walking first" is not the pragmatism of running when one cannot win; it is recognizing that this collision is not a field one should enter, and then walking back onto one's own way.

圣者不兵，隐于宇 Sheng-zhe bu-bing, yin yu yu — the sage has no bing; hidden in the cosmos (the complete form of "persons as ends"). Here the subject changes completely. Not "some higher kind of bing" but the human who has walked out of the sequence of bing. The sage does not need to avoid bing; bing is irrelevant to the sage's state. Being hidden in the cosmos is not hiding; it is existing in a manner that does not need to be seen.

The Deep Structure of the Sequence

This sequence looks like "the level-by-level dissolution of bing as action," but its true deep structure is — how cultivation unfolds ever more purely.

In shang-bing fa-mou, cultivation unfolds through a contest of wisdom — the two sides contend at the level of mou, mou itself is a form of cultivation, and in the contest each side hones deeper judgment in the other.

In chao-bing fa-bian, cultivation unfolds through the mutual revealing of 15DD subjects — in discourse the two subjects shake each other and let each other be revealed, and through this revealing cultivation achieves a deeper self-understanding.

In ji-bing fa-si, cultivation unfolds through self-reflection — the subject no longer keeps its eyes fixed on the other side but draws them back to itself and asks itself why "I have not yet developed enough." This reflection is directly the act of deepening cultivation.

In xuan-bing bu-fa, cultivation unfolds through walking away — recognizing that this field does not belong to one's own path of cultivation, walking back onto one's own way, letting cultivation continue in the right field.

In sheng-zhe bu-bing, cultivation has become the whole form of the subject's existence — there is no longer any position in which "bing" could appear, because cultivation itself fills the subject's entire life.

So the true deep structure of this sequence is — the increasing purity with which cultivation unfolds at different levels. Each level upward, the structural space occupied by cultivation grows larger and the structural space occupied by bing grows smaller. At the level of sheng-zhe bu-bing, cultivation occupies everything, and bing no longer exists.

An equally important formulation — this sequence is the level-by-level redefinition of how "the other side" is constructed:

In shang-bing fa-mou and chao-bing fa-bian, the other side is an object-of-handling. The eyes are on the other.

From ji-bing fa-si onward, the other side is a mirror-for-reflection — that the other wants to collide with me is the other's business; that I have not developed to the point where the collision is meaningless to the other is my business. Responsibility is drawn back to one's own side.

At xuan-bing bu-fa, the other side is part of the field — once the field is recognized, one walks away.

At sheng-zhe bu-bing, the other side is a mutual-chiseling partner — the question is no longer "with whom do I collide" but "with whom do I cultivate together."

The two formulations are two faces of one and the same matter. As cultivation unfolds ever more purely along the sequence, the other side moves ever further along it from "object-of-handling" toward "mutual-chiseling partner." Cultivation needs mutual-chiseling — mutual-chiseling is always needed. But for mutual-chiseling to change its form from "collision" to "growing together," it has to pass through this sequence.

Here one point must be made clear — the end of the sequence is not the absence of collision; it is the absence of unnecessary collision.

Real collision between two subjects — real confrontation, real mutual honing — is a necessary condition of cultivation. Without such collision, subjects wither into self-enclosure and cultivation stagnates. The sage hidden in the cosmos has not gone back to an isolated loop of the self; the sage is still within mutual-chiseling — only the form of mutual-chiseling is no longer war, no longer gui, no longer competition. What is removed is unnecessary collision, not collision itself.

This is the complete form, in the context of war theory, of the core criterion SAE laid down from the very beginning — cultivation grows in mutual-chiseling.

War's Position Relative to the Sequence

War (the object constrained by this treatise's four theorems) is not within the five-level sequence above.

War is the backstop extreme state slid into when all five levels of the sequence have failed. The five levels are the different ways in which cultivation unfolds in situations of collision; war is the means of repair when cultivation has been interrupted. It is not a glorious position within the sequence; it is a protective mechanism outside the sequence that cannot not be activated so that cultivation may recover.

This makes war's position clear — war's relation to the sequence is that of a protector, not a substitute. War does not substitute for the upward sequence; war gives the upward sequence a chance to recover when it has been interrupted. When a subject's cultivation has been interrupted by the expansion/oppression of 14DD systemic purpose, and no level of the five-level sequence can be started in the present (because cultivation itself can no longer continue), war appears as the backstop, its purpose being to restore cultivation to a state in which the upward sequence can continue.

For "all five levels have failed," this treatise has one standard only: fa-mou and fa-jiao must be exhausted through actual attempts, and a path that has not been tried does not count as failed; a path that the other side has structurally closed (for instance, where gui has closed the upward path; see §12) also counts as failed; and even then, the backstop is activated only when cultivation can no longer continue in any non-war form.

Most of SAE's wisdom in handling collision should be spent on the five levels above. War theory handles only this backstop outside the sequence. This treatise introduces the terms chao-bing fa-bian, ji-bing fa-si, xuan-bing bu-fa, and sheng-zhe bu-bing, but does not unfold them (it only names them and characterizes them directionally; see §24) — their unfolding is left to possible future directions of SAE's work.

Across Azeroth the complete spectrum of this sequence can be seen. The Pandaren of the Wandering Isle divided into two schools of thought — the Tushui (committed to compassion, patience, discipline) and the Huojin (committed to action over inaction) — two directions that distinguish themselves through choice within the same mother-culture and, at the moment they parted ways, did not deal with each other in the form of war; this is an approximate form of chao-bing fa-bian, cultivation continuing through the mutual revealing of subjects' convictions rather than through war. The Draenei (an ancient race from the planet Argus), having recognized that their home world could no longer serve as a field in which they could survive as subjects, were led away by their prophet Velen, a few hundred of them aboard the vessel Genedar, and spent thirteen thousand years in exile across the cosmos — a long-scale demonstration of xuan-bing bu-fa, zou wei xian, cultivation continuing in a new field by walking away. Pandaria itself, during the Sundering ten thousand years ago, was shrouded in mist by its last emperor, Shaohao, at the cost of his own life (this mist was bound up with his pride; see §22), so that from the outside it seemed to vanish — an Azerothian version of yin yu yu, cultivation deepening continuously for ten thousand years through not being seen. War appears only at the moment when all these upward paths have failed — as the protective backstop that cultivation cannot not accept.

6. The Isomorphism of War and Law

War and law occupy isomorphic positions in SAE.

Both are protective mechanisms of cultivation. Law handles ordinary collisions — disputes, conflicts, and disagreements between two subjects are handled within the framework of law, and the handling does not involve eliminating either subject by violence; instead, the two subjects reach some resolution on the premise of recognizing each other's subjectivity, so that cultivation can continue after the collision.

War is the continuation at the point where law fails. Or, more precisely — war arises in this situation: a collision that law ought to have handled cannot be handled by law for some reason (law has not yet been established, law has already collapsed, one party refuses to enter the framework of law, the nature of the collision exceeds what law can handle), and so the protection of cultivation is forced to proceed by the cruder and costlier means of war. The failure of law meant here is confined to the situation of 14DD systemic purpose's expansion/oppression: the 14DD party attempts to use 14DD systemic purpose to absorb or destroy the 15DD party's subjectivity, and the 15DD party cannot continue as a subject without resistance. The conventional means of handling — negotiation, arbitration, diplomacy — must also have been exhausted through actual attempts (for the standard of "failure," see §5).

Understanding this isomorphism matters, because it immediately tells us — war is not the opposite of law; war is law's counterpart on the same layer. Both are protective tools of cultivation; they differ in the form of collision they handle (ordinary state / extreme state), and from this follows the difference in how finely they handle it and at what cost. Law is cultivation's ordinary-state protective tool; war is cultivation's extreme-state protective tool.

Neither is cultivation itself. Cultivation proceeds on the home-ground of the Kingdom of Ends — development, mutual-chiseling, the deepening of subjectivity, the continuous growth of a civilization as a whole. Law and war are both tools activated when the home-ground encounters obstacles. A tool must not usurp the place of what it serves.

The isomorphism also tells us one more thing — the Kingdom of Ends ought to make every effort to keep collisions at the level law can handle, and not let them slide to the level of war. For although war as a tool also has a legitimate place in the Kingdom of Ends, its cost is enormous, the wear it inflicts on the very subjects who use it is enormous, and the temporary interruption of cultivation it causes also lasts longer. Law handles collisions without interrupting cultivation; war handles collisions only with a temporary interruption of cultivation (even though its aim is to let cultivation recover). This is the fundamental difference in cost between the two.

SAE does not study how to conduct war well — how to fight more skillfully, how to win more decisively, how to institutionalize war as a legitimate practice. That would elevate war into an object of positive development. War is an extreme-state tool the Kingdom of Ends must sometimes activate; it is not an object of improvement. What requires improvement is cultivation itself — deepening cultivation in peacetime is the best way to reduce both the probability and the severity of war (see §10).

7. War Is a Means; the Goal Is to Make Cultivation Possible

This section draws a dividing line of principle.

Under SAE, war is a means, not an end. The goal of the means is — to make cultivation possible. This looks like common sense, but common sense is extremely easy to lose while a concrete war is under way, so it must be stated explicitly.

The fundamental difference between a means and an end is this — an end has a completion state of its own that can be pursued; a means does not.

The end is cultivation itself — subjectivity continuously deepening, unfolding, growing together. Cultivation has no endpoint (the subject is always growing), but it has a completion state of its own (at any given moment the subject is in growth); for a subject to strive toward cultivation is to keep itself in growth.

War has no completion state of its own. War's "victory" is not war's own completion; neither is "conquering the other," nor "eliminating the other." Under SAE none of these constitutes the completion of war as a means — because for war as a means, its completion state always lies outside war — at the moment cultivation recovers.

Where, then, is war completed? At the moment the "cannot-not" that existed at the opening of war is dissolved — that is, the moment cultivation regains the conditions to continue. When cultivation can proceed again, the war is complete. War's completion lies in the recovery of cultivation outside war itself, not in any state internal to war.

Once war begins to point toward itself — begins to pursue "the war going well," "fighting beautifully," "taking more cities," "eliminating more of the other side" — at that moment war is elevated from means to end. It slides from war that serves cultivation to Kingdom-of-Means war that interrupts cultivation. When war is elevated to an end, it usually goes under one of these names: war as honor, war as national spirit, war as civilizational mission, war as historical necessity.

This slide is not gradual or quantitative — it is completed in that moment, and it is structural. So throughout the whole course of a war, the position of "serving cultivation" must be held unchanged. Once war points toward itself, it is no longer war in SAE's sense — at that moment it has already begun to interrupt cultivation rather than protect it.

Every aspect of a war — its initiation, its continuation, its expansion, its ending — must pass this criterion: does this aspect still serve the recovery of cultivation? Whichever aspect no longer serves it has already slid from means into end. For the reasons for war that fail this criterion, see §13, "Reasons for War That Are Excluded."

8. The Objective Function of War: Do Not Lose, Not Win

This section lays down the treatise's core statement of the objective function.

War's primary direction is not losing, not winning.

"Not losing" is defined under SAE as — not letting one's own side slide, during war, from protector of cultivation to interrupter of cultivation. Concretely, it means not violating the four theorems this treatise lays down below.

The deeper meaning of this definition needs unfolding. War as a means has the protection of cultivation as its reason for existence (§7). At the opening of war, the war-party stands in the position of "protector of cultivation" — it uses violence to clear the way for cultivation, so that cultivation can recover after being oppressed. But in the course of war, the war-party slides very easily from "protector of cultivation" to "interrupter of cultivation" — when it begins to pursue its own expansion, when it begins to take war itself as its end, when it begins to use wartime logic to oppress its own side's subjectivity. Once the slide is complete, it is no longer protecting cultivation but interrupting it — interrupting its own civilization's cultivation. This is "real losing."

Military victory and defeat are decided by other factors: the balance of forces, the strategic environment, the quality of intelligence, chance events, the level of command, luck. The combination of these factors decides the military outcome of a concrete war. Philosophy does not intervene in these factors — philosophy gives direction, not quantitative prescription; concrete strategic command belongs to the domain of military practitioners.

Qualification in principle is another dimension. Whether a war still serves cultivation in principle is decided by the four theorems. This is the dimension philosophy can judge.

The four theorems are the formal test. In everyday terms the test takes a plain form — what the war-party must keep asking is not "how do we win?" but: Are we still the cultivation-protecting party we were when the war began? Are our people still subjects? Is our cultivation still growing?

The two dimensions are separate. A war can fail militarily yet be qualified in principle — a protector of cultivation can lose a qualified protective war; a war can also succeed militarily yet already be unqualified in principle — a war that is "won" may, under SAE, already be "real losing" — military victory accompanied by the interruption of cultivation. Military defeat, loss of territory, even the enemy achieving its war aims — none of these is yet losing in SAE's sense, so long as cultivation itself has not terminated (§19).

What is real losing? It is one's own side beginning, in the course of war, to interrupt its own civilization's cultivation. Once this slide is complete, then even if one wins victory after victory on the battlefield, territory keeps expanding, and the other side keeps falling back, this war is already lost under SAE. For the cultivation one's own side set out to protect has been interrupted in one's own hands.

There is also a kind of losing that points toward the other side: when the means used go so far that the other side's post-war cultivation becomes structurally impossible, the post-war world has lost a possible partner for mutual-chiseling. This kind is tested by Theorem Three's Methods-Constraint Bridge (§15).

This objective function looks negative — its first concern is not losing rather than winning. But it is negative because it must be. Winning is a military goal, decided by the concrete conditions of a concrete war; philosophy cannot guarantee it. Not losing is a goal of principle — not letting cultivation be interrupted in war — decided by one's own side's choices of action; philosophy can supply its criterion. Philosophy gives what it can give, and does not pretend to give what it cannot.

Azeroth offers a typical demonstration. For a period, the Horde Warchief Garrosh Hellscream won victory after victory militarily — expanding territory, breaking Alliance strongholds, and striking the Alliance city-state of Theramore a devastating blow with a large-scale magical weapon (the mana bomb). Measured by the military and territorial dimensions, this was "winning." But in the same period, the Horde's internal cultivation was comprehensively interrupted — he suppressed dissent within the Horde (attempting to assassinate Vol'jin, leader of the Darkspear trolls, so that internal subjectivity could no longer grow), launched purges against the races within the Horde outside his own inner circle (placing the Darkspear trolls under military control, interrupting that race's cultivation), and maintained his rule through a mechanism of terror (enforced by a guard called the Kor'kron, keeping the whole Horde in fear and unable to live normally). Each military victory of Garrosh's Horde was at the same time a further deepening of the interruption of the Horde's internal cultivation. He won many battles, but the cultivation of the Horde as a civilization had already been ruined in his hands. His military victory was real losing.

This example is not meant to put Garrosh on trial. In his position, he made the judgments he could make. But the later resistance against him from within the Horde — the Darkspear Rebellion — was precisely the Horde's act of self-correction once it realized that its cultivation had been interrupted by its own Warchief. In the end, the Horde and the Alliance jointly besieged Orgrimmar (the Horde capital) and overthrew Garrosh from the position of Warchief. This is the structural refutation of the mistaken objective function "winning the fight equals winning" — by overthrowing its own victor, the Horde restored its own civilization's cultivation.

9. Liberating Subjectivity: For Cultivation to Recover

This section lays down a statement of direction — but the statement is placed on the locating layer, not the theorem layer. The reason must first be made clear.

The direction of war — or, if war can point toward anything at all, what it points toward — is the liberation of subjectivity. Liberation has two layers of objects: the defense of one's own side's subjectivity, and opening the possibility for the release of the oppressed subjectivity within the other side. On how oppressed subjects within the other side, or in a third party, are recognized, see §13, the second face — which cannot by itself constitute a reason for opening war.

The deeper meaning of liberating subjectivity is — letting cultivation recover. An oppressed subject has had its cultivation interrupted — it can no longer grow, no longer take part in mutual-chiseling, no longer deepen. The structural meaning of liberating this subject is to let its cultivation continue. So "liberating subjectivity" is not the endpoint of war — war goes as far as liberation, but liberation itself is not completion; the recovery of cultivation after liberation is the completion.

This is the positive meaning war may have under SAE. But this positive meaning is a direction, not a theorem.

The difference between a direction and a theorem lies at the level of grammar. A theorem uses the grammar of constraint — "cannot-not" — which constrains the war-party's actions. A direction uses the grammar of orientation — "points toward" — which gives the war-party an orientation. The two grammars must not be mixed. The grammar of constraint tells you what you may not do; the grammar of orientation tells you where to look while you act.

If "liberating subjectivity" were written as a theorem — "war must be opened for the purpose of liberating subjectivity" — a dangerous gap would open at once: any war-party could justify opening war by claiming to be "liberating subjectivity." Countless colonial wars, crusades, and righteous wars in history have used language of this kind.

So liberating subjectivity can only be a direction, not a theorem. It can be used to describe what a war already opened in a qualified way on other grounds should orient toward as it proceeds — toward letting cultivation recover. It cannot be used on its own to justify opening war.

This point matters greatly for the reader. The four theorems that follow in this treatise are all constraints, and all begin with "cannot-not." Liberating subjectivity appears as a direction on the locating layer, not on the theorem layer. The two must not be confused.

10. Not Fighting Is Also Preparing for War: Deepening Cultivation in Peacetime

Peacetime is a state the War Treatise cannot leave unaddressed. Not fighting is not a blank period — not fighting is the period of letting cultivation deepen in peacetime. This is the real meaning of preparing for war.

Preparing for war in SAE's sense is not the preparation of military force. Preparing military force is the work of military practitioners. Preparing for war in SAE's sense is letting cultivation keep deepening in peacetime:

One, keep the channels of fa-mou and fa-jiao open. In periods without war, dialogue, negotiation, and contest between subjects should go on continuously — these are themselves part of cultivation. Let collisions have a channel of handling before they reach the level of war, and let cultivation keep unfolding through collision rather than being interrupted by it.

Two, maintain mutual recognition of subjectivity. In everyday dealings, the two or more parties keep affirming one another's standing as subjects, and do not let the narrative "the other is the enemy" harden, psychologically or structurally. Mutual recognition is itself cultivation — subjects keep growing in being recognized.

Three, and this is the most fundamental layer — maintain the health of subjectivity within one's own society, letting cultivation keep deepening within one's own side. A society whose internal cultivation keeps deepening is not easily hijacked by war narratives, and not easily triggered by external threats into turning itself into a Kingdom of Means. For such a society is becoming stronger, deeper, and healthier every day — and this continuous deepening is itself the best preparation for war.

This third layer runs exactly opposite to the Kingdom of Means' preparation for war. The Kingdom of Means prepares for war by mobilizing society into a wartime state — and this mobilization is itself the process of society sliding from cultivation toward the suppression of cultivation. A society that remains for long in a state of war-preparation (in the Kingdom-of-Means sense) has already interrupted its own cultivation before war actually arrives.

SAE's preparation for war runs the other way: the more a society lets cultivation keep deepening in the time of not fighting, the thicker its foundation as a subject of cultivation when war actually arrives. This is not a paradox; it is structural logic. Deepened cultivation remains in wartime as the society's underlying ground, carrying the society through the wartime interruption and letting it recover quickly after the war. A society without the ground of deepened cultivation slides at once toward the Kingdom of Means in wartime, and cannot recover after the war either.

So under SAE, the real meaning of the saying "not fighting is also preparing for war" is — every act of deepening cultivation in peacetime decides whether the future will reach the step of "cannot-not," and, if it does reach that step, how thick the society's foundation of cultivation will be.

This also gives "deterrence" a limited reading under SAE: depth of cultivation is the foundation on which any deterrence stays credible over the long run. It does not make 14DD systemic purpose set down its expansionist intent — 14DD, when blocked, only goes dormant (§5); and deterrent war is still unqualified (§13).

In Azeroth's history, the Alliance of Lordaeron (a multi-racial alliance led by the human kingdoms) was founded after the first orcish invasion and took on the task of resisting the second. But in the peace after the Second War ended, the deepening of cultivation within the Alliance did not continue — the high-elven kingdom of Quel'Thalas was the first to withdraw, and then Stromgarde and Gilneas (which cut itself off from the outside world behind a wall called the Greymane Wall) withdrew or drifted away in turn. Mutual recognition among the Alliance's members gradually weakened, and interaction among subjects within it diminished. When the Third War (the invasion of the undead Scourge and the Burning Legion) came, the Alliance could no longer respond in unity as it had in the Second War. When cultivation does not keep deepening in peacetime, the foundation in wartime is thin. This is an example of failed structural preparation in the time of not fighting — not a failure of military preparation, but a failure to deepen cultivation.

11. War and Development: The Form of Cultivation Under Non-Oppressive Conditions

This section answers an implicit question — if war is the extreme-state backstop, where is the home-ground of the Kingdom of Ends?

In cultivation — and the form cultivation takes under non-oppressive conditions is called development.

This is not a substitution of words. Development is the concrete manifestation of cultivation in a state of freedom. When subjectivity is released, cultivation grows naturally; when subjects recognize one another, cultivation deepens naturally; when a civilization is not interrupted, cultivation accumulates into visible fruits of development — the all-round flourishing of science, art, technology, organization, thought, and aesthetics. All of these are the outward appearance of cultivation.

The final contest between the Kingdom of Ends and the Kingdom of Means is not decided in war; it is decided in the deepening of cultivation. This is a structural statement and needs careful explanation.

The Kingdom of Ends releases cultivation. The release of cultivation activates a positive feedback loop of creativity, judgment, and the capacity to cooperate, and this loop brings a faster pace of development — because development is simply the natural accumulation of cultivation when it is not interrupted. The Kingdom of Means suppresses cultivation. Suppression cannot activate this loop — oppressed subjects either lower their output in passivity, or consume social resources in resistance, or lose the capacity for long-term planning in fear. The gap in development speed between the two kinds of regime accumulates exponentially.

Over the long run, the Kingdom of Ends prevails with high probability. Note that this is high probability, not necessity.

SAE does not promise historical necessity. It is not certain that barbarism cannot conquer civilization; this has happened again and again in human history. A civilization with deeper cultivation may, at some specific historical juncture, be destroyed by a civilization that temporarily defeats it. The possibility of failure is real, and this possibility is not within this treatise's object. This treatise says only one thing about it: that cultivation cannot not continue is an ontological statement, not a prediction of how history will turn out, and so it does not lose its force because failure is possible; how commandments are rewritten from "ought" into ontological statements of "cannot-not" is the work on the ontological commandment (Reason Cannot Not: An SAE Refinement of Kant's Four Formulations of the Categorical Imperative, DOI 10.5281/zenodo.19751512).

But this high-probability prevailing is already enough for us to draw an important conclusion — war is not the home-ground of the Kingdom of Ends; cultivation (and its form in a state of freedom: development) is.

If the survival of the Kingdom of Ends depended on victory in war, the Kingdom of Ends would already be at a structural disadvantage — because the Kingdom of Ends is not good at war (its mechanisms of cultivation must be partly restrained in wartime, or it cannot form unified war action). If the survival of the Kingdom of Ends depends on the continuous deepening of cultivation, the Kingdom of Ends holds a structural advantage — because deepening cultivation is precisely the Kingdom of Ends' own proper work.

So the direction of this treatise is — to let the reader, having finished it, turn attention back to cultivation, and not let attention linger long on the theme of war. The War Treatise is a necessary piece of work, but it is not itself the core work of cultivation. Cultivation is.

The history of the Draenei is a witness on a long scale. Before their native world Argus was conquered by the Burning Legion (a trans-dimensional civilization of destruction), their prophet Velen had already recognized that the situation was beyond saving — their people had been seduced and split into two factions, and the faction that accepted the Legion's power (led by Kil'jaeden and Archimonde) could not be persuaded. Velen's choice was neither to fight (they were no match for the Legion) nor to surrender (surrender meant the whole race being thoroughly remade into the Legion's weapons), but to walk — taking several hundred of his people who were willing to leave, he boarded the Genedar, a ship of the Naaru, and left Argus. They spent thirteen thousand years in exile across the cosmos, and each time the Legion caught up with them, they walked again (including more than two hundred years on Draenor, where the orcs slaughtered them; see §19). The core of those thirteen thousand years was not military preparation but the continuous deepening of cultivation — they learned the wisdom of the Naaru (beings of Light), deepened their spiritual tradition, and built the capacity to remain subjects even in flight. When at last they reached Azeroth, they did not come back by military counterattack; through thirteen thousand years of deepening cultivation they became one of the core races of the Alliance, and in the end a key force in the counteroffensive against the Legion. War was not the home-ground of the Draenei — the continuous deepening of cultivation was.

12. Mou · Yin-mou · Yang-mou · Gui

This section handles a technical issue central in Sun Tzu's Art of War but requiring repositioning under SAE — the technical branching within the level of bing.

Even at the level of bing (the direct object of this treatise), technical orientation is not binary (legitimate / illegitimate) but forms a four-level spectrum: mou, yin-mou, yang-mou, gui. The branching among the four is not a branching of moral choice; it is a branching of structural position — their relations to cultivation are completely different.

Mou — Both Sides Can Use It, Legitimate

Mou (谋, strategy) is wisdom, calculation, stratagem, positioning. That both sides in a collision use mou is normal — letting collision unfold as a contest of wisdom actually reduces harm.

Shang-bing fa-mou — what Sun Tzu saw centrally: collision should be directed toward the level of mou, not stay at the cruder level of brute force. When the two sides contest at the level of mou — whoever sees farther, judges more accurately, prepares more thoroughly — these real capabilities decide the outcome. Mou does not oppress one's own side's subjectivity, and it does not operate through deceiving the other — it is a transparent contest of wisdom.

Mou is fully legitimate under SAE. It directs collision toward the genuine display of both sides' capabilities, and so reduces unnecessary harm.

Yin-mou — Dependent on Deceiving the Other, Mediocre

Yin-mou (阴谋, scheming) obtains advantage by concealing true intent, manufacturing false appearances, causing the other to misjudge. Feinting east to strike west, bluffing strength, luring the enemy in deep — all belong to this level. Yin-mou depends on "the other not knowing my true plan."

Short-term, it may work. But yin-mou has a structural weakness — once seen through, it fails, and the other permanently keeps guard against all one's postures. All future cooperation, agreements, and signals get discounted. The short-term gain of yin-mou does not make up for the long-term collapse of trust.

Yin-mou is common in collisions of 14DD systemic purpose against 14DD systemic purpose; both sides may use it, and both pay the long-term cost. Yin-mou is not completely prohibited by SAE, but it is the "mediocre" technique — its long-term structure is less solid than that of yang-mou, its legitimacy less clear than that of mou.

Yang-mou — Truly Effective

The approach of yang-mou (阳谋, open strategy) is — placing the plan in the open, and even so, the other cannot but follow.

The other knows fully what one is going to do, one's bottom line, one's next move. But the other cannot prevent it — because one's structural position (resources, time, geography, technology, real capability, legitimacy) makes the matter advantageous to oneself, and even foreseeing it, the other has no usable counter.

Yang-mou's strength comes not from information asymmetry but from the structural position-differential itself.

This makes yang-mou unique under SAE — it is the cultivation-friendly technique of war. Both sides take part in the collision in full clarity, neither side's subjectivity is canceled, and the outcome is determined by structure rather than by information asymmetry. The other, as a clear-sighted subject, accepts the structural verdict — which is itself a form of cultivation. The other is convinced in clarity by structure, not defeated in blindness by information asymmetry.

Yang-mou has a further, deep property — time is on yang-mou's side. Real structure does not need maintenance (the real is automatically consistent with itself); falsehood requires continuous maintenance (every new statement must stay consistent with every prior statement; every external verification is a point of risk for being seen through). Yang-mou rests on a real structural position-differential, so the yang-mou side's cost falls over time; if adversaries try to counter yang-mou through yin-mou or gui, their maintenance cost rises monotonically over time. Yang-mou does not win in the short term; yang-mou wins in time.

The phrase "great gui resembles sincerity" (大诡若诚) really points toward the highest form of yang-mou. Gui pushed to the extreme paradoxically walks out of gui and becomes yang-mou — because gui at the extreme requires appearing sincere, and sincerity at the extreme becomes transparency; but once transparent, the source of strength must shift from "packaged appearance" to "structure itself." If the structure itself has no advantage, transparency only exposes weakness; if the structure itself has advantage, transparency becomes yang-mou. Yang-mou is not some super-gui; it is what remains once the gui axis has vanished — the natural display of structural advantage.

In Azeroth, a complete demonstration of yang-mou is Varian after the Siege of Orgrimmar. When the Horde's civil strife and the siege of its capital Orgrimmar ended, the Alliance forces had in fact already broken into the Horde capital. Voices within the Alliance argued for seizing the moment to dissolve the Horde completely (the proposal came from Jaina Proudmoore). But Alliance High King Varian Wrynn chose otherwise — he put everything in the open.
