By then the Horde had already raised Vol'jin as its new Warchief on its own, and Thrall had knelt and pledged to follow him; only then did Varian learn who the successor was, and he said to Vol'jin: "The Horde has committed heinous crimes, Vol'jin. But some among you fought against Garrosh's tyranny. For that, I am willing to end this bloodshed. But know this… If your Horde fails to uphold honor, as Garrosh did… We will end you." Then the Alliance withdrew from Orgrimmar.

This is yang-mou — Varian stated his judgment, his bottom line, and the consequences completely in the open. The Horde knew everything, but this did not constitute oppression of the Horde. Varian's words had force because real structural capability stood behind them — the Alliance genuinely could end the Horde if necessary; the statement required no maintenance because it was true. The Horde, as a clear-sighted subject, accepted this structural verdict — not deceived, not blinded, but seeing the situation in full clarity. How the Horde then went on was the Horde's own decision.

Gui — Oppressing One's Own Side's Subjectivity, Illegitimate

A definition guarding against misreading must be laid down here, because "gui" in everyday language is easily read as "deception in war" in the broad sense.

What this treatise calls "gui" (诡, deception) is not any tactical concealment or camouflage before the enemy, but the war-technique that operates at the cost of oppressing one's own side's subjectivity and cutting off internal questioning.

A one-off tactical concealment on the battlefield (ambush, feigned attack, disguised march route) is not in itself the "gui" this treatise means — that falls under yin-mou or mou, and is a common technique in situations of 14DD systemic purpose against 14DD systemic purpose. What this treatise locks onto as "gui" is a deeper layer — in order to sustain some systemic deception structure for the war-party's overall operation, one's own internal people must be kept from knowing the truth. This structural internal deception is the "gui" this treatise structurally opposes.

Gui's critical point is not deceiving the other (that is yin-mou). Gui's critical point is — for some tactical purpose, the war-party must conceal the truth from its own soldiers, its own people, its own internal ranks.

Letting one's own people become "execution units that do not need to know the whole picture" — this is gui's structural definition. Gui degrades one's own subjectivity into tools; it turns one's internal space into a Kingdom-of-Means structure.

The fundamental reason gui is illegitimate is not that it harms the other; it is that it turns one's internal space into a Kingdom of Means. Every use of gui oppresses one's own subjectivity once more and interrupts one's own cultivation once more.

Gui directly conflicts with Theorem Four (Cannot-Not-Be-Open-to-Questioning). Theorem Four requires the war-party to allow itself to be questioned — to allow its own internal ranks to know decisions, challenge decisions, and demand that decision-makers give an account. Gui requires suppressing internal questioning — keeping one's own people from knowing the whole picture, from challenging it, from having their demands for an account met. The two cannot coexist. So gui is structurally a violation of Theorem Four at the level of execution.

The Four-Quadrant Analysis of Gui

Let us verify gui's structural position in all four directions.

Direction one: 14DD systemic purpose against 14DD systemic purpose — both sides can use gui

An even collision between two civilizations. Both sides still view the other from the standpoint of "the other as an object of handling," neither has genuinely released subjectivity, and both have structural room available for oppressing their own side. In this situation, gui is a usable technique for both. But both pay the cost of their own cultivation being interrupted — gui is a mutually damaging technique. This is what most historical wars look like — both sides using gui, both paying the cost in cultivation, neither reaching a higher position.

Direction two: 15DD against 15DD — gui can neither be used nor be of use

Collision between two civilizations genuinely operating at 15DD takes the form of discourse (fa-bian) or walking first (zou wei xian); it does not escalate to war. Even supposing escalation were possible — discourse requires both sides to reveal themselves to each other, and revealing presupposes that one's own side knows internally what its subject-conviction position is. A civilization that conceals things from its own side does not even fully possess its own position, and cannot take part in discourse. So 15DD against 15DD cannot actually come to war at all; gui here can neither be used nor be of use.

Direction three: 15DD against 14DD, the 15DD party (defender-resister) — structurally unable to use gui

This is the deepest direction in the question of gui. The 15DD party as defender-resister cannot use gui — not because it chooses not to, but because its structure makes gui unusable.

Theorem Four requires allowing oneself to be questioned. Gui requires suppressing internal questioning. The 15DD party's soldiers, people, and dissenters can question decisions, and the commander cannot suppress these questions (because suppressing questioning violates Theorem Four and thereby falls away from 15DD). So gui cannot even start operating within the 15DD party's structure.

Nor is the 15DD party's structural aim, as defender-resister, to eliminate the other — it is to let the other (the 14DD oppressing party) truly stand up as a subject. The 14DD oppressing party is oppressing because it is itself instrumentalized by its own systemic purpose (the people within it are likewise oppressed subjects). The real work of the 15DD party's resistance is to give the other the chance to be liberated from its own systemic purpose's instrumentalization. This work cannot be accomplished through gui — gui presupposes the other as an object that can be deceived, while the work of liberation presupposes the other as a subject who can stand up again.

This also lays a key piece of structure for §19 — the 15DD party as resister can neither use gui nor pursue the other's yu-sui (because pursuing yu-sui amounts to giving up on the other as a subject capable of evolving). These two are two faces of the same structure.

Direction four: 15DD against 14DD, the 14DD party (oppressing party) — the principal user of gui

The 14DD party as oppressing party uses gui — and is in fact the principal user of gui. The expansion/oppression of 14DD systemic purpose structurally depends on systematic deception, inward and outward: inwardly, it needs its own instrumentalized subjects not to know the whole picture (not to know the real cost of expansion, not to know they are being used as tools); outwardly, it needs the oppressed party and third parties to misrecognize its nature (packaging itself as "justice," "civilization," "liberator").

Gui is the 14DD party's native technique. Part of why the 14DD party can expand is precisely its skilled use of gui. But gui's cost to the 14DD party itself is equally real —

Cost one: Internal cultivation is completely interrupted — the 14DD party's internally instrumentalized subjects exist long-term in a state of being deceived and oppressed; cultivation cannot occur internally.

Cost two: Short-lived — the "internal instability of 14DD victory" that §19 will develop is already operating here. The 14DD party uses gui to sustain expansion, but gui's maintenance cost rises monotonically over time and eventually exceeds the gain from expansion, and the 14DD party collapses of itself in its internal contradictions. Many large conquest-type structures tend to decline or fragment within a few generations — this is the structural self-destruction of gui as a tool of 14DD expansion.

Cost three: When its soldiers and people see that the opposing 15DD party does not use gui, allows internal questioning, and operates transparently — self-doubt arises. "Why don't they do this over there? Are we really the 'right' side?" This self-doubt slowly accumulates within the 14DD party. The self-doubt is itself deep work of cultivation. Individuals within the 14DD party, through comparison, recognize that gui is the mark of structural disadvantage, and from within demand that their own system stop using gui. This recognition is the critical node for individuals inside the 14DD party to evolve toward 15DD — recognizing gui as the problem is recognizing that one needs to be liberated from instrumentalization by 14DD systemic purpose.

So although gui is a usable technique for the 14DD party, gui is also the 14DD party's structure of self-destruction. The more gui is used, the more thoroughly the 14DD party's own cultivation is interrupted, the lower its own stability, and the greater the tension within it toward evolving to 15DD. Gui is not the 14DD party's long-term advantage; it is its chronic self-destruction.

Gui's Structural Self-Destruction and the Time Dimension

The more gui is used, the greater the friction within one's own side — gui raises the probability of losing through increased internal friction.

At a deeper layer — gui interrupts one's own civilization's cultivation. Cultivation requires mutual recognition, mutual honesty, and mutual trust among subjects. Gui in wartime internalizes internal deception as the basic mode of one's own operation. Once this mode is internalized, it does not automatically disappear after the war — wartime training in gui remains as an internal structure of the civilization, making post-war cultivation impossible to recover.

Time stands against gui. The maintenance cost of gui rises monotonically over time: the number of those in the know grows over time; the risk of keeping fabrications consistent accumulates over time; the number of external verifications grows over time. Each of these pushes gui toward collapse. Yang-mou strengthens over time; gui decays over time. This is the deepest time-asymmetry in collision handling.

Refusing gui is not speaking on behalf of the other side — it is thinking on behalf of the cultivation of one's own civilization. This is a structural observation, not a moral commandment.

In Azeroth, the clearest demonstration of gui's structural self-destruction is the process by which the orcs were bound by the blood pact. On Draenor, the Horde's homeworld (a world of the outer reaches), Kil'jaeden, leader of the Burning Legion (the Burning Legion being a trans-dimensional civilizational organization centered on destruction), worked first through the venerable elder shaman Ner'zhul, using a false "Draenei threat" narrative to convince him that the orcs needed to unite against the Draenei; later he turned to work through Ner'zhul's apprentice, the orc warlock Gul'dan. Gul'dan offered the Legion's power to all the orc clan chieftains on Draenor — drinking the blood of the demon lord Mannoroth. After drinking, the orcs gained enormous fighting strength but were at the same time bound to the Legion's will. The warrior leader Grommash Hellscream drank first, and most chieftains followed. Only a few refused — such as the Frostwolf clan led by Durotan, which clearly recognized that this was slavery, not power. The gui Gul'dan used in this process centered on concealing from the orcs themselves the blood pact's long-term cost, and thereby masking his real purpose (making the orcs into the Legion's tools). The critical point of gui lies in internal concealment — if all the orcs had known the real cost of the blood pact, none would have drunk. Gui gave Gul'dan short-term power, but the structural consequence was that the entire orc civilization's cultivation was completely interrupted. After the Second War, the orcs in the internment camps fell into the double cycle of lethargy (long-term listlessness caused by withdrawal from the blood pact) and demon-crazed madness (in some of them). It took the orcs as a civilization an entire generation (from Gul'dan to Thrall) for their cultivation to begin restarting. A complete demonstration of gui's structural self-destruction — what self-destructs is not only the user of gui, but the entire civilization's cultivation.

Gui Locks Down the Sequence

A final observation — gui not only has structural problems at the level of bing; it also locks down the possibility of the entire upward sequence.

A civilization habituated to concealment from its own side cannot reach any upward level — every upward level requires real mutual-chiseling within one's own side (real revealing between subject and subject). A civilization habituated to internal concealment has even the mutual-chiseling within its own side locked down, let alone real mutual-chiseling with the other.

It cannot reach chao-bing fa-bian, because discourse requires sincerity — including sincerity toward one's own side. It cannot reach ji-bing fa-si, because fa-si requires moving one's gaze from the other back to oneself, while what gui trains is precisely watching the other persistently while concealing internally. It cannot reach xuan-bing bu-fa, because bu-fa requires recognizing the field and walking away, while gui habituates one to staying in the field and operating there. It cannot reach sheng-zhe bu-bing, because bu-bing requires standing in "persons as ends" — including one's own people as ends — while gui stands in "one's own people can be used as tools."

So the real consequence under SAE of the saying "bing is the way of gui" (兵者诡道也) is — compressing the entire possible space of collision handling from the full spectrum of the five upward levels down to the single lowest level, shutting down every upward path of cultivation. A civilization habituated to gui is locked at the lowest level of bing, never able to walk the upward path of deepening cultivation.

This is the deepest reason why "bing is the way of gui" completely fails under SAE. It is not that gui does not work — as a technique it is effective, at least in the short term. It is that gui locks civilizational cultivation at the lowest level. SAE's rejection of gui is not a moral posture; it is in order to keep the upward channel of cultivation open.

This section's treatment of Sun Tzu is not moral evaluation but structural observation.

And here a complete credit to Sun Tzu must be given — that Sun Tzu could separate mou and gui two thousand three hundred years ago is itself already moving in the direction of 15DD.

Sun Tzu established the hierarchy "the highest bing attacks by strategy; next attacks by diplomacy; next attacks by bing; lowest attacks by assaulting cities." The meaning of this hierarchy is that bing is not all one thing. Within bing there is high and low, better and worse, different structural positions. Directing collision toward mou is worth more than directing it toward assaulting cities; fa-mou is placed above fa-bing — the establishment of these distinctions is itself a kind of structural self-awareness, showing that Sun Tzu had already felt that "bing" is not a homogeneous toolbox but a field with an internal hierarchy.

Similarly, Sun Tzu stated "bing is the way of gui" as an observation about war-technique — he did not beautify gui, did not elevate gui into war's highest form. On the contrary, within the hierarchy he established, he placed fa-mou above fa-bing and mou above gui. In his own language, he was already saying that "attacking by mou is better than gui".

To get this far at the top of 14DD is already very deep. It means Sun Tzu had already felt, within 14DD, that — in the business of war not all techniques are equivalent; some techniques are more worth pursuing than others; the highest war-technique is the kind that does not need to fight (fa-mou, fa-jiao). These feelings can be expressed in SAE's language as "orientation toward the mutual recognition of subjects," "orientation toward the preservation of cultivation," "orientation toward the upward sequence" — but Sun Tzu had no such language available. He used the 14DD-top language available to him and said it as far as he could.

This is the seedling within 14DD already evolving toward 15DD. Sun Tzu did not complete this evolution (Kant's second formulation had not yet been laid down in his era; "persons as ends" was not yet a criterion available to him), but he was already walking in that direction. SAE war theory takes up his work, not overthrowing him — but saying fully, in a language that came only later, what he had already felt but could not finish saying.

Sun Tzu saw the difference between mou and gui. What SAE war theory can do is — make the structural reasons for this difference clear: mou does not oppress subjects, gui oppresses subjects; yang-mou lets the other take part as a clear-sighted subject, gui uses one's own side as a tool; the strength of mou comes from a real contest of wisdom, the strength of gui from information asymmetry; time stands with mou and yang-mou, and time stands against gui. This treatise draws on Sun Tzu's distinction of levels to carry out its own structural reconstruction; the fourfold division into mou, yin-mou, yang-mou, and gui, and the classing of the suppression of one's own side's subjectivity as illegitimate, are judgments this treatise takes on under SAE, not claims already completed in Sun Tzu's text.

These structural reasons Sun Tzu could not state, but he felt them. Someone who could feel this two thousand three hundred years ago was, within 14DD, holding up the directionality of cultivation. That we today can state it in SAE's language is standing on Sun Tzu's shoulders — this inheritance is itself one of the ways of cultivation.

Middle Volume · The Four Theorems

The Middle Volume handles the core of SAE war theory — the four theorems constraining war. Together, the four theorems constitute the complete protective mechanism of cultivation in the situation of war.

Before unfolding each, place the four in parallel so the reader sees the whole —

Theorem One · Cannot-Not-Starting

The opening of war must satisfy a cannot-not — that is, fa-mou and fa-jiao are exhausted, subjectivity is pushed to the critical threshold (the continuation of cultivation can no longer be maintained), and not fighting would cause cultivation to be completely interrupted in this field. At the opening of war it must also already be possible to answer how this war is to end, and how cultivation on both sides can continue when it ends (Theorem Three's backward requirement; see §15).

Theorem Two · Cannot-Not-Expanding

Once war has begun, every expansion (expansion of scope, escalation of intensity, increase in participating parties, extension of objectives, prolongation of time, upgrade of means) must pass the cannot-not criterion at the level of Theorem One; "since we've already started fighting" may not be used to smuggle in objective-drift.

Theorem Three · Cannot-Not-Be-Directed-Toward-Ending

Every action, every expansion, every adjustment of war must be directed toward the ending of war — that is, toward the moment when the original cannot-not is dissolved and cultivation regains the conditions to continue. War cannot not be directed toward ending.

Theorem Four · Cannot-Not-Be-Open-to-Questioning

The war-party cannot not be open to questioning. The objects of questioning are the war-party's decisions, actions, direction. Questioning may come from within one's own ranks, from within the other side's ranks, from third parties, from future history. The war-party has no privilege of not being questioned.

The Structure of the Four Theorems:

All four begin with "cannot-not" — this is the shared grammar. "Cannot-not" strips the war-party of sovereign grammar — it is not "I have the right to start the war / expand / refuse questioning," but "I am forced by structure to this point." This shared grammar unites the four at the principled layer.

The four theorems' constraint objects form a penetration-depth sequence —

  • Theorem One constrains the initiation of action (whether to open war)
  • Theorem Two constrains the continuation and escalation of action (every expansion)
  • Theorem Three constrains the direction of action (toward ending)
  • Theorem Four constrains the war-party's own position (being open to questioning)

From initiation to continuation to direction to position, penetrative depth deepens layer by layer. Theorem Four penetrates deepest — it does not constrain some particular action; it constrains the very form of the war-party's existence as a subject.

The four theorems are not a parallel list of norms; they are concentric-ring constraints — each protects, at a deeper layer, cultivation from being swallowed by war itself.

The Position of the Four Theorems Under the Cultivation Perspective:

Theorems One, Two, and Three together constrain war-as-action from interrupting the other's cultivation (abuse of war-opening, uncontrolled expansion, sealing off the other's reorganization — all interrupt the other's cultivation).

Theorem Four simultaneously protects the war-party's own cultivation (a war-party not open to questioning has already interrupted its own internal cultivation; even if it wins militarily, it has lost the internal conditions for post-war cultivation to continue).

Together the four form the complete protection of cultivation in the situation of war — not letting war as a means turn around and swallow the very thing it was supposed to protect. This is the Middle Volume's work as a whole.

Below, the four are unfolded in turn.

13. Theorem One · Cannot-Not-Starting

The beginning of war is a threshold. Once this threshold is crossed, cultivation in the subject concerned must undergo an extreme-state processing — handling collision with violence. Thus this threshold must be laid down very severely — war, as a protective tool, is activated only when cultivation has been oppressed to the point of being unable to continue in the present.

Theorem One's statement: The opening of war must satisfy a cannot-not — that is, fa-mou and fa-jiao are exhausted, subjectivity is pushed to the critical threshold (the continuation of cultivation can no longer be maintained), and not fighting would cause cultivation to be completely interrupted in this field. At the opening of war it must also already be possible to answer how this war is to end, and how cultivation on both sides can continue when it ends (Theorem Three's backward requirement; see §15).

Every word of this statement carries structural function, and each must be examined in turn.

The Structural Force of "Cannot-Not"

"Cannot-not" in this treatise's usage is not the emotional "don't want to but still will." Its structural force is identical to a genuine end — the position that SAE Learning Series Paper 3 laid down for the "cannot-not." What is a genuine end? Something the subject orients toward no matter what; something without which the subject cannot continue as a subject.

Placing this force on war's opening condition means — war cannot be started because "I want to," cannot be started because "there is advantage in it," cannot be started because "the other might pose a threat," cannot be started because "they offended us in the past," cannot be started "for honor." War must be started because not fighting would cause cultivation to be completely interrupted in this subject — not merely temporarily paused (war itself also temporarily interrupts cultivation), but structurally losing the possibility of continuing.

The Three Conditions of the Criterion

One, fa-mou and fa-jiao are exhausted. This is the external condition. Between the two or more parties, all possible paths at the level of mou (wisdom-contest, interest-negotiation, structural bargaining) and at the level of jiao (diplomacy, channels, third-party mediation, the procedures of international institutions, non-military pressure, the concluding and repairing of agreements) have been tried, and all have failed. Not tried once or twice and given up — genuinely exhausted, no non-violent path available in the existing structure. Fa-mou and fa-jiao are themselves forms of cultivation — subjects continue growing through mou and jiao. Exhaustion means the continuation of cultivation in these gentler forms has become impossible.

Two, subjectivity pushed to the critical threshold. This is the internal condition. One's own position as subject has reached the point where, without resistance, it will fail — meaning the carrier of cultivation itself is about to be eliminated. Note that what is meant here is the risk that the carrier of cultivation fails to hold, not "interest loss," "face loss," or "a future that may be insecure" in the general sense. The carrier of cultivation failing to hold means — the very identity of being a subject cannot structurally continue; cultivation is not merely interfered with but loses the position in which it happens.

Three, an ending can be envisioned. This is the forward-looking condition, traced back from Theorem Three (see §15). At the moment of opening war, one must already be able to answer: if this war is fought, how is it to end? When it ends, how can cultivation continue on both sides? This is not a demand to foresee the whole future; it is a demand for a sense of the direction in which cultivation is to recover. An ending in which the other side ceases to exist as a subject of cultivation does not count as an answer (the Methods-Constraint Bridge, §15).

All three conditions must be met simultaneously. If only the first is met (fa-mou and fa-jiao exhausted) but subjectivity has not reached the critical threshold, cultivation can still continue through other means — forbearance, concession, trading time for space, seeking other paths. If only the second is met (subjectivity at the critical threshold) but fa-mou and fa-jiao have not been exhausted, paths for cultivation to continue in non-war situations remain to be tried. If only the first two are met and no one can say how this war is to end, then the directionality required under Theorem Three has not yet been established, and opening war is blind — directed not toward the recovery of cultivation but toward some uncertain future. How the second condition is recognized in the intervention case is set out under Gate Four in this section. When this treatise calls the opening of war, an expansion, or some wartime action "legitimate" in the sense that it passes a theorem's criterion, the word is synonymous with "qualified"; the other two uses of "legitimate" — the source of legitimacy of war as a means, and the legitimacy of mou — fall outside this.

Reasons for War That Are Excluded

Under this criterion, the following common reasons for war all fail to qualify:

Preventive war ("The other might threaten us in the future, so we strike first") — future possibility is not present necessity: a threat that lies in the future has not caused one's own cultivation to fail to hold in the present, so the second condition is not met.

Deterrent war (fighting one war as a warning to other potential adversaries) — using war as a tool for sending signals, with the other's soldiers as the objects through which the signal is transmitted, has already structurally instrumentalized the other — and this in itself interrupts the other's cultivation.

Interest-driven war (for resources, territory, trade rights: "Because this is in our strategic interest") — acquiring interests is not defending cultivation. Applying the language of protecting cultivation to a contest over interests is a typical rhetorical slide.

War over old grievances ("They offended us in the past") — historical offense does not establish current structural impossibility of cultivation. To fight in order to settle an old grievance is to treat war as an end, not as a means.

War for honor (over a humiliation, an affront, an instance of unfair treatment) — damage to honor is a matter of the 14DD layer; it does not touch the continued existence of cultivation itself. To justify war by honor is to let 14DD swallow the very criterion that should protect cultivation.

War on grounds of cost ("It would be too costly not to respond now") — cost calculations belong to mou-level reasoning; they do not meet the threshold of cannot-not.

War on grounds of capability and right ("We have the capability, and the right as well") — neither capability nor right is a cannot-not. Having the means in hand does not establish the cannot-not.

War to spread one's system ("Our system is better and should be spread") — even if one's own system genuinely protects cultivation better, forcing it on the other through war violates the very "persons as ends" principle the system is supposed to embody. §18's surrogate-liberation firewall develops this point.

Pre-emption ("The other is about to attack us, so we strike first") — this requires strict distinction. If the other's attack is actually under way, it is no longer pre-emption; it is resistance. If the other's attack is still only "about to" happen, the case falls back into the problem of preventive war.

All these excluded reasons belong to the Kingdom-of-Means grammar of sovereign decision. SAE strips them all.

This list is not exhaustive. But its function is to show the concrete direction in which the criterion applies — any reason for war must pass the three conditions of the criterion; a reason that does not pass is unqualified.

The Two Faces of Defending Subjectivity

A structure must be specially unfolded here, because it is easily misread.

The "defense of subjectivity" under Theorem One has two faces — the defense of one's own subjectivity and the recognition of oppressed subjectivity within the other side. The two faces are structurally connected, but their standing must be distinguished with great care.

The First Face: Defending One's Own Subjectivity

This is the trunk of Theorem One. When one's own subjectivity is pushed to the critical threshold by another party's 14DD systemic purpose, defending one's own subjectivity is a legitimate reason for opening war. This face is direct and explicit; it is the core situation to which SAE war theory applies.

The Second Face: Recognizing Oppressed Subjectivity Within the Other Side

Sometimes the situation in which war is opened is not only one in which one's own side is oppressed: within the other side there are oppressed subjects too — some clan, some stratum, some group whose subjectivity is likewise at the critical threshold under the other side's own 14DD systemic purpose. Does this situation constitute a reason for opening war?

The answer is — the second face can only be seen alongside a first face that already holds; it cannot by itself constitute a reason for opening war.

The same applies when one party oppresses a third party: war cannot be opened for that reason alone. The third party's oppression is the third party's own first face; an outsider can stand alongside it only when the outsider's own first face holds, and cannot open war on its behalf.

The second face does not set up the two further gates of "non-war paths exhausted" and "proportionality of means." Whether non-war paths are exhausted belongs to the criterion's first condition, which the intervention side must pass all the same; whether means are proportionate belongs to the expansion criterion of §14 and the Methods-Constraint Bridge of §15.

Four extremely strict gates against abuse must be in place here:

Gate One: Cannot Be Started Alone

The second face cannot become a stand-alone reason for opening war. A subject cannot open war across a border because "another country oppresses some people inside it." To do so is to make war a tool for solving others' problems in their place — and this substitution is itself another kind of replacement of the oppressed's subjectivity. Countless historical "wars of liberation" and "humanitarian interventions" began their slide into imperial logic with the collapse of this gate.

Gate Two: Seeing Is Not Substituting

Even where the first face holds, the recognition of oppressed subjectivity within the other side must keep the posture of "seeing" and must not slide into the posture of "substituting." An external subject does not decide for the other's people how their subjectivity should be realized. I can recognize your plight, recognize that as a subject you ought to be released, but I do not decide for you what you are to become, I do not choose your leaders for you, I do not plan your future for you.

Gate Three: The Oppression and the Rising Must Be Genuine

The oppression must be genuine, not a casus belli manufactured for opening war: it is real and ongoing, and it is actually interrupting the cultivation of the oppressed — not merely policy disagreement, not ordinary governance, and not the intervention side's projection of its own standards onto the other.

When we say "oppressed subjects within the other side are rising," this rising must be a genuine self-declaration from within the other side — not an illusion manufactured from outside, not a resistance that exists only under external aid, not something constructed to supply legitimacy for one's own war. Genuine rising proceeds from the inside out; the outside can recognize it and can support it, but cannot manufacture it.

Gate Four: The Intervention Side Should Have Its Own Stake in the Matter

Gate Four tests whether the intervention side's first face is genuine — the stake it speaks of is precisely the erosion of the intervention side's own cultivation, now under way; such ongoing erosion counts as the first face holding. This is how the first face is recognized in the intervention case. The critical threshold that the criterion's second condition requires is, in the intervention case, borne by the party being stood beside: its own carrier of cultivation is being pressed to the critical threshold (the definition of the second face; Gate Three). The intervention side itself need only be genuinely drawn in: its own cultivation is being eroded now, and this erosion comes from the same source as the oppression the other party suffers, the expansion of one and the same 14DD systemic purpose. So in such a war there is always a party at the critical threshold, and that party has itself risen (Gate Three); the intervention side stands beside it and does not open war on its behalf. The half that says "ongoing, not predicted" is not relaxed (see below). The criterion's first and third conditions, fa-mou and fa-jiao exhausted and an ending can be envisioned, the intervention side must pass all the same; the four gates do not stand in for these two tests.

Completely "selfless" trans-border intervention is, if anything, structurally suspicious — because selfless intervention has no cannot-not of its own, and thus no position to stand on when it comes to the opening-war criterion. An intervention side with a genuinely qualified position of intervention typically has its own stake in the collision (the other's 14DD systemic-purpose expansion also threatens the intervention side; the other's expansion will spread to the intervention side; the other's internal oppression, spilling over, structurally affects the intervention side's maintenance of subjectivity — under Gate Four, each of these must be erosion already under way and from the same source as the oppression suffered by the party being stood beside). Stake is not a stain but a healthy anchor — it lets the intervention side's action be accountable to its own criterion, rather than pretending to stand on "pure justice."

But the "stake" here must be precisely limited — not expansionist interest (territorial expansion, resource acquisition, market expansion), but structural stake in which the intervention side's own cultivation is already implicated. Specifically: if the intervention side does not act, its own subjectivity-position will be harmed; its own cultivation will be interrupted by the other's 14DD systemic-purpose expansion; its long-term conditions for survival as a civilization will be threatened by the other's oppression structure. Such stake is one's own cultivation-position implicated, not "I can also gain benefit from it" — all three of these must be continuations of erosion already under way and from the same source as the oppression suffered by the party being stood beside; see below.

An even harder limitation must be laid down — this stake must be an ongoing structural erosion happening at this very moment, not a future probabilistic risk derived from predictive models.

This distinction is critically important. Because "future probabilistic risk" is the breeding ground for all preventive-war rhetoric — "they will threaten us in the future," "if we don't fight them, our civilization will be affected," "by the time they grow strong it will be too late." Such rhetoric can package any expansion as defense, can disguise 14DD systemic purpose as 15DD defense. The line is drawn here: the intervention side must point to erosion that is ongoing (actions already on its territory, structural oppression already affecting its people, concrete mechanisms already interrupting its cultivation) — not to risk that may occur. Intervention based on "future risk," however persuasive, does not satisfy Gate Four.

This limitation shuts preventive-war logic out at the gate completely. An intervention side genuinely protecting its own cultivation can point to ongoing facts; an expansion side pretending to protect its own cultivation can only point to predicted possibilities. The line between the two is clear.

This limitation is important because in real political discourse "one's own stake" is easily stolen by expansionist logic — any expansionist war can claim "we also have a stake." Gate Four's "stake" is not this expansion-packaged false stake; it is the genuine structural implication that arises when the intervention side's own cultivation home-ground has itself already been affected.

Azeroth Example: The First Face

The resistance of the continent of Pandaria is the typical demonstration of Theorem One's first face.

The Pandaren civilization of Pandaria is an ancient civilization cut off from the rest of Azeroth ten thousand years ago; one of the cores of its existence is maintaining a spiritual state unpolluted by emotion (its land has long harbored creatures called the Sha, embodiments of negative emotion, and the core of Pandaren culture is learning not to let one's own emotions feed them). During one period, the Horde Warchief Garrosh Hellscream brought war into Pandaria and dug into the spiritual center of Pandaren civilization — the Vale of Eternal Blossoms — in search of the power of an ancient artifact (the Heart of Y'Shaarj, the heart of a sealed-away Old God). This act directly polluted the heart of the Pandaren civilization's spiritual structure.

It was at this point that Pandaria's counterstroke began. But the nature of this counterstroke was not "retaliation for invasion" — the Pandaren were capable of bearing a considerable degree of external disturbance; their peacetime preparation existed precisely to absorb external shocks without being drawn in. The counterstroke began at the moment the civilization's spiritual heart was directly attacked, and the core condition for the subject's continued existence as a subject was destroyed.

This is precisely the deep form of the first face — not defending the main military position, but defending the spiritual structure on which subjectivity relies in order to be a subject.

Here one can see the severity of Theorem One. Before this, Pandaria had borne a great deal of invasion, plundering of resources, and harm to native species — none of it triggered full counterattack. Only when the spiritual heart was directly destroyed did the counterstroke begin. The threshold of cannot-not is set extremely high.

Azeroth Example: The Second Face

The orc uprising is a compound demonstration of Theorem One's second face.

In Azeroth's history, the orcs were for a period held in internment camps in Lordaeron, sunk in the long listlessness of blood-pact withdrawal (the blood pact was the poisonous pact by which Gul'dan, through gui, bound them to the will of the Burning Legion; see §12). An orc named Thrall escaped from enslavement under the human slave master Blackmoore (who had raised him as a slave gladiator for the arena), reconnected with his own people, learned the long-lost shamanic way of his ancestors, and finally, together with the old warrior Orgrim Doomhammer, launched the campaign to liberate the internment camps.

The structure of this campaign contains both the first face and the second, and all four gates are satisfied:

First face: After breaking free of Blackmoore, Thrall was still one of the orcs the Alliance's internment system meant to hunt down and send to the camps; the clans still at large outside the camps and their kin inside them stood within the same oppression. His uprising was first of all for the group to which he himself belonged.

Second face, Gate One (not started alone): Thrall's uprising was not purely for the external reason of "liberating all oppressed orcs" — he himself was one of the oppressed. The second face was not started as a stand-alone reason for war.

Second face, Gate Two (seeing, not substituting): Thrall liberated the camps, but did not "substitute" for the orcs in deciding what they would become afterward. The orcs liberated from the camps made their own choice to join the new Horde. No one chose for them.

Second face, Gate Three (oppression and rising genuine): The oppression in the camps was real and ongoing. The rising of the liberated orcs was genuine. Their lethargy in the camps was not the disappearance of their subjectivity but the outward symptom of blood-pact withdrawal. Once the oppression was lifted, subjectivity returned of itself — this is precisely the individual-level appearance of the SAE axiom "subjectivity cannot be eliminated; the remainder cannot not develop."

Second face, Gate Four (the intervention side has a stake): The old warrior Doomhammer was a close friend of Durotan, Thrall's father and chieftain of the Frostwolf clan (Doomhammer himself belonged to the Blackrock clan and had once been Warchief of the Horde). He himself was also one of the orcs this oppression targeted — the camps and the manhunts were aimed at the orc people as a whole. His stake was real and ongoing: his own cultivation was being eroded in this very oppression, and under Gate Four this counts as his first face holding. His intervention was not "purely selfless salvation"; it was a joint action standing in the same structural position as Thrall.

The four gates govern only the safeguards against abuse of the second face; whether the uprising as a whole is qualified under Theorem One still requires each of the three conditions to be examined, and all three are judged as of the moment the uprising began. The second condition is carried by the first face: the camps and the hunt were aimed at the orcs as a whole people, and the carrier of cultivation of the group Thrall belonged to was being pressed to the critical threshold. As to fa-mou and fa-jiao, this treatise reads lifelong internment (§18) and the continued pursuit of orcs outside the camps (the first face, above) as a system that offered the orcs no path to peaceful release and autonomous reorganization — this is the treatise's structural judgment about the situation at the uprising's outset, a path structurally closed by the other side (§5). Later, before attacking Durnholde, Thrall still proposed a parley, and Blackmoore answered by killing Taretha; this was a continued probing of the fa-mou and fa-jiao window before an expansion, followed by rejection (§14, condition three). It is read under §14 and does not retrospectively supply an attempt before the first use of force. As to the envisioned ending, freeing his people and ending their internment gave the uprising a limited direction, one that does not take as its ending that humans cease to exist as subjects of cultivation; the terms proposed after Durnholde — freeing the remaining orcs, setting aside land, and trade — gave this direction a concrete form: dissolution of the threat, followed by separation and coexistence (§15). On this structural judgment about the outset and this limited direction, the treatise judges Thrall's uprising to pass Theorem One.

Note the contrast here — if at that time some external power (say, some human kingdom) had unilaterally launched a war to "liberate the orcs" because "the orcs are so pitiable," that war would be unqualified under Theorem One. It starts the second face alone; it would slide into substitution; the rising would not necessarily be genuine (manufactured from outside rather than risen from within); the intervention side lacks its own stake (or its stake is fabricated). If any one of the four gates gives way, the second face degenerates into the rhetoric of imperial logic.

Thrall's uprising is qualified not because it is "morally correct" — as said earlier, SAE does not make moral judgments — but because it structurally passed all of Theorem One's criteria. Qualification is a structural judgment, not a moral one.

14. Theorem Two · Cannot-Not-Expanding

That a war has begun is only the first step of structural judgment. Once a war has begun, it is not a static state — it changes, expands, spreads, escalates. No change automatically carries forward the qualification that came before; every expansion must pass the criterion at the level of Theorem One anew.

This is the core statement of Theorem Two.

Under the cultivation perspective, Theorem Two is Theorem One extended in time. Theorem One protects against "abusing the tool of war at the opening of war"; Theorem Two protects against "letting the tool of war, while the war is under way, swell into a force that interrupts cultivation." Once war begins, it has its own momentum — it tends to continue, to expand, to find new objectives. If this momentum is not continuously constrained, war turns from a protective tool of cultivation into an interrupter of cultivation. Theorem Two is this constraint.

Theorem Two's statement: Any expansion of war — whether expansion of scope, of intensity, of participants, of objectives, of time, or of means — must pass a cannot-not on its own: without expanding, the position defended under Theorem One would fall, and the recovery of cultivation would become impossible.

The Multiple Dimensions of Expansion

Expansion is not a one-dimensional concept. A war can expand along several dimensions:

Expansion of scope — from a local battlefield to a wider geographic area.

Expansion of intensity — from limited engagement escalating into total war.

Expansion of participants — bringing in allies, drawing in third parties, turning into a multilateral conflict.

Expansion of objectives — from the concrete objective originally defended to larger purposes.

Expansion of time — from short-term conflict prolonged into long-term war.

Expansion of means — from conventional means escalated to unconventional means.

These dimensions are not exhaustive — they are listed so that the reader sees that the structure of expansion is many-sided, not to supply a checklist. Expansion along any one dimension must pass the criterion on its own.

The Internal Logic of Escalation

Why does war tend to escalate even when the expansion cannot pass the cannot-not criterion? The momentum just mentioned can be broken down into four mechanisms:

Sunk-cost fallacy at civilizational scale — the war-party has already paid costs; further escalation is justified by the demand that those costs "not be wasted."

Mobilization inertia — the mobilization machinery built for the war has its own momentum and interests, which push it to keep operating.

Public emotional commitment — public narratives built to support the war demand ongoing enemies and ongoing objectives.

Adversary dynamics — each side's escalation triggers the other's escalation, producing a spiral neither side chose from scratch.

Theorem Two is there to hold out against these four mechanisms. Every expansion must be judged anew, regardless of how far the war has already gone.

The "Cannot-Not" Criterion for Expansion

The structure of the criterion is: without expanding, the position defended under Theorem One will fall.

This criterion is isomorphic with the criterion for opening war, but what it constrains is different. The criterion for opening war asks "is opening war, here and now, a cannot-not?"; the criterion for expansion asks "is expanding, at this very moment, a cannot-not?" Both must cut through surface reasons such as "it looks advantageous," "it looks worthwhile," "it looks like what we ought to do," and reach straight to the conditions for the continued existence of subjectivity itself.

Concretely, a qualified expansion must meet three conditions at once:

One, it is directed toward the dissolution of the original "cannot-not". This condition has two layers:

The originally defended position is falling — at the war's current stage, without expanding, the "cannot-not" of the war's opening cannot be dissolved, and the loss of the position will become fact.

The direction of expansion still serves the original defensive objective — expansion is not for gaining more, not for "since we're fighting anyway, let's fight a bit more," not for "settling other problems along the way." Expansion must point directly at the objective of dissolving the very "cannot-not" that existed when the war was opened.

Two, with prior investment erased, the expansion, proposed afresh, still reaches the level of Theorem One. At every decision point one must ask: if this expansion were being proposed fresh today, with none of the war's prior investment counted, could it pass Theorem One's criterion — including being able to say how this war is to end after the expansion? If the honest answer is no, the expansion is unqualified. The prior investment in the war is not a justification; it is a sunk cost that must be set aside in the evaluation.

Three, before expanding, there is an obligation to keep probing the fa-mou and fa-jiao window — even while war is under way, the probing of non-war paths should not stop. Before expanding, one must confirm that non-war paths remain unworkable.

If what appears is a new "cannot-not" unrelated to the original objective, it does not count as an expansion of this war; it is an independent application of Theorem One in a new round, and must pass Theorem One on its own from the start.

The Smuggling of Objective-Drift

A particular trap must be guarded against here — the drift of the war's objective in the course of the war.

At its start, a war has a clear "cannot-not." As the war proceeds, the war-party may, without noticing, slide the objective from the original "cannot-not" onto something else. The most common slides are:

Drift into elimination. A war to defend subjectivity against falling becomes a war to eliminate the other as a subject.

Drift into territorial acquisition. A war of defense becomes a war of conquest once early military advantage is gained.

Drift into regime change. A war to stop a specific oppression by the other side becomes a war to replace the other side's entire government. This is exactly the "choosing your leaders for you" that §13's Gate Two forbids.

Drift into civilizational transformation. A war to stop a threat becomes a war to restructure the other side's culture, institutions, or way of life. The aim was to make the other stop its oppression; now it is to make the other into the "correct" shape.

Drift into a once-and-for-all settlement. A war to resolve the present conflict — to deal with this one "cannot-not" — becomes a war to resolve every possible future threat for good.

Drift into prolonged occupation. A war that has achieved its original objectives, and should now be at its ending, continues under the rationale of "stabilization" or "preventing recurrence."

None of these drifts constitutes a qualified expansion; they are simply transgressions of the war's objective. Once the transgression has occurred, however much more "thorough," more "efficient," more "done in one stroke" it may look militarily, under SAE it is already an unqualified expansion.

Azeroth Example: Unqualified Expansion

The Theramore incident is the typical case of "unqualified expansion" under Theorem Two.

In Azeroth, the Horde Warchief Garrosh launched an attack on the Alliance city-state of Theramore (Theramore Isle, a diplomatic stronghold led by the human archmage Jaina Proudmoore). The structure of expansion in this attack deserves step-by-step analysis.

First layer of expansion: from attacking Northwatch Hold (an Alliance outpost) to besieging the city of Theramore itself. This layer has, on its face, one reason: if the attack on Northwatch did not press on to Theramore, the original strategic objective might not be attained. But failure to achieve a strategic objective does not by itself establish a cannot-not for expansion; the original war's qualification must first be established, and the three conditions in this section must then be examined independently — this case has neither step. Moreover, a warning sign already appears here — after Northwatch fell, Garrosh had his army halt and did not press on. This halt itself may mean that the criterion for "continuing to expand" no longer held (what this halt was really for is taken up in the second layer).

Second layer of expansion (a concealed objective): After Northwatch fell, Garrosh deliberately held his forces back, drawing the Alliance to move its fleet, commanders, and mages to Theramore to defend the city; the siege that followed was a lure from the start, the mana bomb had been settled in advance, and it was dropped as soon as the Horde withdrew. Baine and Vol'jin learned only afterward that the Horde had never meant to win. So this layer is not objective-drift in the middle of the war; it is a true objective concealed from one's own side from the outset: what was put before the Horde's chieftains was a siege; the true objective was to draw the Alliance's fleet, commanders, and mages into one place and destroy them at a stroke, and even the Horde's own chieftains did not know it. As a result, no layer of expansion was ever honestly put to judgment under Theorem Two — one objective was presented, another was carried out, and the true objective never faced the criterion. The siege as a lure was, toward the Alliance, a feigned attack, which falls under yin-mou (§12) and is not what is being judged here; what makes this layer the gui of §12 is that the true objective was concealed even from the Horde's chieftains, who had standing to question it — not a one-off tactical secrecy, but part of the structure that §16's second layer describes, in which the real decisions were made in secret. The chieftains' objections were promptly suppressed (see below), which connects this in turn to Theorem Four in §16.

Third layer of expansion (upgrade of means): Garrosh used an enormous mana bomb (the Mana Bomb, a large-scale magical weapon on a scale approaching that of a nuclear weapon) to level the entire city of Theramore in an instant, killing hundreds of defenders, mages, and civilians. The use of this means passed no criterion at all.

The key structural fact: two chieftains within the Horde — Vol'jin, leader of the Darkspear trolls, and Baine Bloodhoof, leader of the tauren — both raised objections. Baine opposed the plan to Garrosh's face at the council before the siege; Garrosh threatened him with his father's fate, and Malkorok stepped in front of Garrosh; Vol'jin at first kept silent and objected only when Garrosh spoke of driving the Night Elves out of Kalimdor. Garrosh suppressed the questioning with the Warchief's authority.

This suppression is itself a violation (Theorem Four: Cannot-Not-Be-Open-to-Questioning), and at the same time it exposes the failure of the entire expansion process under Theorem Two — a qualified expansion can withstand questioning; an unqualified expansion often has to suppress questioning in order to proceed.

Here the two theorems cross-verify each other.

Azeroth Example: Qualified Expansion

Set against the Theramore incident is the expansion within Thrall's uprising.

Thrall's uprising began on a very small scale — only the liberation of the first internment camp under Blackmoore's control. The expansions that followed proceeded like this:

First expansion: from one internment camp to a second and a third. The reason — the Alliance shared camp-management intelligence internally; the liberation of the first camp gave the orcs in the other camps hope of liberation, but it also led the management to strengthen the guard at the other camps. Without continuing the liberation, the hope already kindled would be extinguished under harsher repression. This expansion passes the criterion.

Second expansion: from liberating camps to attacking Durnholde Keep (Blackmoore's military command center). The reason — Blackmoore systematically deployed his knights to the remaining camps to intercept the liberation. Without destroying the command center, the remaining camps could not be liberated. This expansion passes the criterion.

A third expansion — turning to the conquest of Lordaeron — did not happen.

After taking Durnholde and killing Blackmoore, Thrall did not turn the liberation of his people into the conquest of Lordaeron. Destroying the internment system's command center did not mean that the orcs in the remaining camps had all been freed; whether the remaining internment still constituted a cannot-not requiring war, and whether and how further action could proceed, remained subject to the criteria under the circumstances at that time. The victory already won supplied no authorization for "conquering Lordaeron," "taking revenge on the humans," or "building an orc empire." He sent word to the Alliance through an intermediary: the remaining orcs were to be freed and land set aside for their use; if the Alliance did not attack, the orcs would not attack either, and they were willing to trade; if the Alliance chose to fight them, it would make itself an enemy the likes of which it had never seen. Later (during the Third War), he led his people across the sea to Kalimdor to rebuild.

This example shows that a local victory is no reason to move beyond the limited aim of liberation. An unfinished part of the original liberation task and a new objective of conquest must be judged separately; the latter cannot borrow authorization from the former.

Compared with Garrosh at Theramore, the difference between the two structural orientations is immediately clear — Garrosh, throughout his expansions, kept looking for "a bigger objective"; Thrall, throughout his expansions, kept looking for "the boundary of the cannot-not." The former is the expansion logic of the Kingdom of Means; the latter is the constraining logic of the Kingdom of Ends.

15. Theorem Three · Cannot-Not-Be-Directed-Toward-Ending

A war that has begun, and has expanded in a qualified way, faces its next structural question: is it being conducted in the direction of ending?

This is what Theorem Three states at its core.

Under the cultivation perspective, Theorem Three's position is the most critical. Theorem One lets war be opened in a qualified way (the protective tool is activated only when cultivation has been oppressed to the critical threshold); Theorem Two keeps war from swelling (cultivation is not interrupted in the course of expansion); Theorem Three ensures that war, as a means, actually stops once its work is done — so that cultivation obtains the space to recover after the war. This theorem structurally constrains war's sense of direction: every action of war must point toward the moment when "cultivation can continue again." A war that does not point toward this moment, even if nominally still under way, has under SAE already degenerated — sliding from a means of protecting cultivation into an inertia that interrupts it.

Theorem Three's statement: Every action, every expansion, every adjustment during war must be directed toward the moment of ending — the moment when the "cannot-not" that existed at the opening of war is dissolved. War cannot not be directed toward ending.

The Methods-Constraint Bridge

Before Theorem Three is unfolded, a bridge sentence must first be laid down, hanging whether the specific methods used in war are qualified directly on Theorem Three.

The Methods-Constraint Bridge: methods that would pre-empt the "possibility of ending" that Theorem Three requires, or that would make the other side's post-war cultivation structurally impossible, are in principle unqualified.

This means that certain war-methods violate Theorem Three by their very use — because these methods structurally seal off the form of ending, that is, they seal off the possibility of cultivation recovering after the war. At the moment such methods are used, Theorem Three is violated — not because the methods are cruel or inhumane (these are ethical judgments SAE does not make), but because the methods structurally destroy the direction "toward cultivation recovery."

Specifically, which methods structurally seal off the possibility of cultivation recovery?

Large-scale non-discriminating killing — if one attack eliminates, at a stroke, the material and human base for the other side's post-war recovery as a cultivation-carrier, the other can no longer exist as a cultivation-subject; the war will not end at the moment "cultivation can continue again" but at the moment "the other as cultivation-carrier has disappeared." These two ending-forms are completely different — in the former, war still serves cultivation; in the latter, war has become the terminator of cultivation.

Precision targeting of subject-reconstruction capacity — for instance, the one-shot elimination of the other side's leadership, cultural core, educational core, spiritual core. Even if the casualty numbers of such targeting look "not large," if its effect is that the other can no longer organize itself as a cultivation-carrier, it also violates the Methods-Constraint Bridge. Cultivation needs its load-bearing structures — culture, education, spiritual inheritance — to continue. Destroying these structures equals destroying cultivation itself, even if the physical carriers remain.

Irreversible structural transformation — for instance, contaminating an area so that it becomes uninhabitable for the long term, or destroying a natural or cultural heritage that cannot be rebuilt. The "victory" of such methods is permanent; there is no possibility of "recovery," and so no possibility of cultivation unfolding again.

The Precise Boundary of the Bridge

The bridge must be given a precise boundary here, to prevent it from being read too broadly.

The bridge does not prohibit all actions that weaken the other. Attacking the other's current oppressive capability, current warfighting capability, current military organization — these may be structurally qualified (if they serve the dissolution of the "cannot-not").

The bridge prohibits attacks that, beyond what is needed to dissolve the cannot-not, directly destroy the minimal conditions for the other's post-war reorganization as a cultivation-subject.

Put differently: if the effect of an attack is limited to "dissolving the other's current oppression of one's own cultivation," it is permitted by Theorem Three; if the effect extends to "eliminating the minimal conditions for the other to stand up again as a subject after the war," it violates the bridge.

The boundary is here — current oppressive capability vs. minimal conditions for post-war subject-reconstruction. The former may be attacked; the latter may not.

This boundary makes the bridge operational. It is not "any critical target is excluded"; it is "attacks that exceed what is needed to dissolve the cannot-not and destroy future cultivation possibility are excluded." The space of judgment for military and political leaders in specific situations is defined by this boundary.

The SAE Definition of Ending

The meaning of "ending" under Theorem Three needs to be precise.

The moment the "cannot-not" that existed at the opening of war is dissolved is the moment the war ends — that is, the moment cultivation regains the conditions to continue.

This definition does not include the common end-states of war such as "the other surrenders," "the other is eliminated," "the other accepts our terms." Those states may accompany the ending of a war, but they are not themselves the SAE definition of ending. Ending under SAE is cultivation again able to proceed between the two or more sides — the situation that had made cultivation unable to continue has disappeared.

Why does this definition matter? Because it anchors the ending of war outside the war — in the recovery of cultivation — rather than in the state of winning or losing inside the war.

Winning and losing inside the war are a matter of the 14DD layer; whether cultivation has recovered, outside the war, is a matter of the 15DD layer — it is the judgment of whether war, as a means, has really completed its protective work. Because Theorem Three anchors ending at the 15DD layer, it can judge whether a war is still, in principle, serving cultivation.

The "cannot-not" can be dissolved in several ways:

  • Dissolution of the threat — the expansion/oppression of the 14DD systemic purpose has ceased, or has been defused.
  • Separation — once separated, the parties can coexist, no longer locked in the relation of expansion and defense. Separation carries two qualifications: after separating, each side's own cultivation can continue; and the possibility of later handling collision within cultivation is not sealed off. "Each side's own cultivation can continue" means that separation itself makes neither side's cultivation structurally impossible; oppression inside the other side is not thereby counted as resolved. Separation is a matter between the war-parties, not a sorting or confining of persons: it does not decide for either side's people who belongs where or who must move where (Gate Two; §18, Obligation Two), and it is not maintained by shutting people in on one side.
  • Mutual recognition — both sides, through the war or in parallel outside it, pass into a relation in which cultivation can continue.

These ways describe how the "cannot-not" is dissolved; they are not the same classification as the three ending forms of the Lower Volume (victory, failure, equilibrium). If the other side is merely dormant after being blocked (§5), separation dissolves this cannot-not, yet by the distinctions of §20 it may still be only a truce.

The Difference from Kingdom-of-Means "Ending War by War"

SAE's view of ending differs fundamentally from the Kingdom of Means' "ending war by war."

Kingdom-of-Means ending war by war: reaching some "permanent peace" through war. The structure of this peace is that one side prevails completely, and the other completely disappears or is transformed until it no longer has the capacity to resist. In this ending the war is over, but usually the other also disappears as a cultivation-subject — in this "permanent peace," the other's cultivation is permanently terminated as well.

The SAE ending: the "cannot-not" of the war's opening is dissolved, the other still exists as a cultivation-subject, and cultivation becomes possible again between the two sides. After the war, the two sides still need to keep handling collision — only no longer through war, but within cultivation.

The difference between the two views of ending is not technical; it is one of principle. Kingdom-of-Means ending war by war pursues a state of "no more other," which at the same time means "our own cultivation also loses a mutual-chiseling partner"; SAE pursues a state of "the other still there, but the cannot-not dissolved," which lets both sides' cultivation continue. The former is usually unstable over the long run (the suppressed subjectivity returns in new forms, and accumulated resentment distorts the cultivation of the suppressing side as well); the latter is usually stable over the long run (both sides continue to exist as cultivation-subjects, collision can be handled by non-war means, and cultivation can deepen on both sides).

The Backward Requirement on the Decision to Open War

Theorem Three carries a forward-looking requirement — the envisioning of how cultivation will recover must begin at the opening of war.

A subject, at the moment of deciding whether to open war, must already be able to answer these questions: If this war is fought, how will it end? When it ends, how can cultivation on both sides continue? What state will the other be in after the war? What state will we ourselves be in after the war? How is the "cannot-not" to be dissolved so that cultivation can recover?

If these questions cannot be answered, the directionality required under Theorem Three has not yet been established, and opening war is blind — directed not toward the recovery of cultivation but toward some uncertain future. Such blind opening of war structurally and necessarily violates Theorem Three, and is almost certain to interrupt the cultivation of both sides.

The backward effect of this requirement on the decision to open war is enormous — a war without a clear conception of how cultivation will recover should not be opened. This does not mean that every future must be foreseen; it means there must be a sense of direction: once this war has been fought, in which direction will cultivation recover? A war without a sense of direction almost certainly loses its direction in the course of fighting, and in the end interrupts not only the other's cultivation but one's own as well.

This requirement has therefore been written into the statement of Theorem One, as the third condition of Theorem One's criterion — an ending can be envisioned (see §13).

The Single Ground of War's Existence as a Means

The deep structure behind Theorem Three is this — war, as a means, has no independent reason to exist.

This echoes what §7 said — "war is a means; the goal is to make cultivation possible." War can be a legitimate act of the Kingdom of Ends because it serves the recovery of cultivation. Once cultivation has the conditions to continue, the source of war's legitimacy disappears.

So war, as a means, must at every moment point toward its ending — toward the moment cultivation recovers. A war not directed toward the recovery of cultivation has, at that moment, already lost its legitimacy as a means — because it has no independent reason to exist; if it is not directed toward cultivation's recovery, it is maintaining itself, perpetuating itself, and such self-maintenance is structurally the slide from protector of cultivation to interrupter of cultivation.

Ask "How Do We End This?" — Not "How Do We Win This?"

Theorem Three's force is that the direction toward ending must operate at every moment of the war, not just at the end. Every decision, every escalation choice, every negotiation move must ask: does this move us closer to the conditions for ending?

A war conducted under Theorem Three is a war constantly asking itself "how do we end this?" — not "how do we win this?" The difference is enormous. Asking "how do we win" pushes toward maximal pressure, maximal damage, maximal subordination of the other. Asking "how do we end" pushes toward the minimum necessary to dissolve the cannot-not, toward preserving post-war reconstruction, toward finding the moment when the conflict can be de-escalated.

Azeroth Example: The Paradigm of Ending

The Alliance withdrawal after the Siege of Orgrimmar is the paradigm of Theorem Three.

At the close of the Siege of Orgrimmar (Orgrimmar is the Horde capital; the siege was a campaign conducted jointly by the Alliance and the rebel forces within the Horde), the structure was this: Garrosh had been captured; his authority as Warchief had collapsed; his oppression of the Horde's other races no longer existed; his military threat to the Alliance had been dissolved. By then the "cannot-not" of the war's opening — the double threat that Garrosh as Warchief posed to both factions — had been dissolved.

At this moment, two lines of argument appeared within the Alliance:

Jaina Proudmoore (one of the survivors of Theramore) argued for using the opportunity to completely dismantle the Horde. Her reasoning was moral — the Horde had committed crimes at Theramore and elsewhere and should pay for them.

Alliance High King Varian Wrynn made the choice of withdrawal. On this treatise's reading, his reasoning was structurally precise: Garrosh's threat had been dissolved; the original "cannot-not" no longer existed. Advancing further, occupying Orgrimmar, dismantling the Horde — these were new actions, which would have had to pass a new criterion to be qualified. And these new actions were unqualified under any criterion — the Horde's other races had been oppressed third parties under Garrosh, and had already joined the siege as allies; continuing to wage war on them would be new 14DD systemic-purpose expansion/oppression, the Alliance itself sliding toward the very structure it had been resisting.

Varian's final words to the Horde's new Warchief Vol'jin (the Darkspear troll leader who had survived Garrosh's assassination attempt and led the rebellion within the Horde) — "The Horde has committed heinous crimes, Vol'jin. But some among you fought against Garrosh's tyranny. For that, I am willing to end this bloodshed. But know this… If your Horde fails to uphold honor, as Garrosh did… We will end you." — are a complete demonstration of yang-mou (§12). Everything is placed in the open, the bottom line is clear, the consequences are clear, yet this does not constitute oppression of the Horde.

The war ends here. Not "the Horde is eliminated," not "the Horde is transformed," but Garrosh's threat is dissolved, and the Horde still exists as a subject. This meets the SAE definition of ending.

Varian's reasoning was later set out in Blizzard's official short story A Good War, through the recollection of the old soldier Saurfang (an elder of the Horde) — Varian knew how many lives continuing the advance would cost, and he chose not to make his people pay that price. On the surface this reasoning is "a weighing of costs," but underneath it is Theorem Three itself — continuing the advance was no longer directed toward ending; it was directed toward a new expansion that no criterion had tested. In that choice, Varian held to Theorem Three.

Azeroth Example: A War That Lost Its Direction

Set against the ending of the Siege of Orgrimmar, Garrosh's war in Pandaria is the typical case of a war that has lost its direction.

Garrosh's invasion of Pandaria had no clear reason for opening war in the first place — his knowledge of the continent was limited, and his motive surfaced only gradually, in the course of events, as a vague "the Horde must control this place." Once he was in, the war's objective kept changing:

First, control of the Divine Bell (an ancient artifact that could strengthen soldiers' fighting power). After the Divine Bell was destroyed, the objective became excavating the Vale of Eternal Blossoms in search of the remains of an Old God. Each objective arose as the natural next step because the previous one had failed or had already been obtained; not one was checked back against the "cannot-not" of the war's opening.

This is the typical case of a war that has lost its direction — every step is "the next step," and no step is "the ending."

The result could be foreseen — such a war cannot end by its own internal logic, because from the start it had no orientation toward ending. It can only be interrupted from outside. And it was in fact interrupted from outside — the rebellion within the Horde (the uprising against Garrosh led by Vol'jin), together with the Alliance counteroffensive, brought this war to an end. Garrosh himself never intended to end it.

Azeroth Example: The Methods-Constraint Bridge

The Theramore mana bomb serves as a boundary test of the Methods-Constraint Bridge.

The mana bomb killed at a single stroke: most of the Alliance's 7th Fleet, which had come to the city's aid; Rhonin, leader of the Kirin Tor, and a number of mages who had come with him; Kinndy Sparkshine (Jaina's apprentice); and those in the city who had not got out in time.

The consequence of this strike was that the Alliance lost the leader of the Kirin Tor and a number of its mages. This was not an ordinary battlefield loss; it was a strike that severely weakened the Alliance's capacity to go on existing as a subject of magical power, and it made the Alliance's post-war reorganization extremely difficult.

The destruction of Theramore must be examined under the Methods-Constraint Bridge. But severely weakening the Alliance's magical capacity and making post-war reorganization extremely difficult do not by themselves establish that its post-war cultivation is structurally impossible. If the Alliance is taken as the other side, the argument must go on to identify which irreplaceable minimal condition for reorganization was destroyed; until that step has been argued, this example cannot be called a complete proof of the bridge.

The use of the mana bomb remains unqualified, for the reason given in §14: it passed no cannot-not (the third layer of expansion). This example simply no longer serves as a complete demonstration of the bridge.

So what this example shows is the bridge's threshold of proof: severe weakening and difficult reorganization do not yet amount to structural impossibility. Any method whose use would make the other's post-war reorganization as a subject structurally impossible violates the bridge, however "effective" it may be militarily; but to judge a method so, one must identify which irreplaceable minimal condition for reorganization it destroyed.

16. Theorem Four · Cannot-Not-Be-Open-to-Questioning

The first three theorems constrain the actions of war (opening, expanding, the direction toward ending). Theorem Four constrains the state of the war-party itself as a cultivation-subject.

Under the cultivation perspective, Theorem Four's position is the deepest — it protects the war-party's own cultivation. The first three theorems mainly keep war from interrupting the other's cultivation (abuse of war-opening, uncontrolled expansion, an ending that seals off the other's reorganization). Theorem Four keeps the war-party from interrupting its own internal cultivation during war — by suppressing dissent, eliminating questioning, deifying decision-makers, and the like. A war-party that does not allow itself to be questioned has already interrupted its own internal cultivation; even if it wins militarily, it has already lost the internal conditions for cultivation to continue after the war.

Theorem Four's statement: The war-party cannot not be open to questioning. The objects of questioning are the war-party's decisions, actions, direction. Questioning may come from within one's own ranks, from within the other side's ranks, from third parties, from future history. What "cannot not be open to questioning" means is this: The war-party has no privilege of not being questioned.
