Questioning is itself a structural need of cultivation. The unfolding of cultivation within a subject requires continual reflection, disputation, correction — a subject cannot go on cultivating in a state of not being questioned; that state is self-enclosure, not growth. Questioning keeps the subject in a position of reflection even in the extreme-state situation of war, so that even in wartime cultivation is not wholly drowned by the logic of war.

The structural standing of this theorem differs from that of the first three. The first three are norms — they tell the war-party how it ought to act. Theorem Four is at once a norm and a diagnostic — it requires the war-party to allow the position of being questioned, and it also serves as an externally observable mark by which onlookers can directly judge whether a war, in principle, serves cultivation.

Theorem Four as the Diagnostic for the Other Three

Let us make this special standing clear.

Under the first three theorems, the war-party can make its own claims. It can say "we have exhausted fa-mou and fa-jiao," "our expansion is necessary," "we are directed toward ending." Such claims are hard to verify directly from outside — because outsiders cannot see the war-party's real internal process of judgment. To verify them, outsiders would have to understand the war-party's internal decision-making in depth, and in war such in-depth understanding is usually impossible.

Theorem Four is different. Whether questioning is allowed is directly observable from outside.

Is anyone in the war-party questioning? What becomes of those questioners? Do the results of questioning actually affect decisions? These are all facts visible from outside. A Kingdom-of-Means war-party can forge passage under the first three theorems, but it can hardly forge a real allowance of questioning — because a real allowance must be embodied in structure: questioners exist, questioning reaches the decision core, questioners are protected, questioning actually affects decisions. Each of these four leaves traces visible from outside.

So Theorem Four structurally serves the first three in this way: it is the external-observable diagnostic for whether the first three theorems are actually being observed. When an onlooker cannot directly judge whether a war is qualified under the first three theorems, the onlooker can look at Theorem Four — if the war-party passes under Theorem Four, the probability that it also passes under the first three is high; if the war-party fails under Theorem Four, the probability that it passes under the first three is extremely low.

This diagnostic standing means that, in analyzing any war, Theorem Four should be the first diagnostic point. Not because Theorem Four is more important than the first three, but because the information it provides is the easiest to obtain from outside and the hardest to forge.

Theorem Four as the Concretization of SAE's Core Criterion

Theorem Four's standing in SAE war theory carries a deeper implication still — it is the concretization of SAE's overall criterion, "structure over claim."

SAE has never drawn the line between the Kingdom of Ends and the Kingdom of Means at the level of claims — any Kingdom of Means can claim to act "for the people," "for justice," "for liberation." SAE judges at the structural level: does this subject's operating structure treat persons as ends? Or does it, structurally, use persons as means?

Theorem Four is this criterion made concrete in the domain of war. A war-party's claim that "we are fighting a just war" carries no weight under SAE — what SAE looks at is whether the war-party structurally allows itself to be questioned. For allowing questioning is, at the structural level, treating questioners as subjects; not allowing questioning is, at the structural level, treating questioners as tools (their capacity to question is suppressed, their standing as judging subjects is canceled).

So Theorem Four's standing in war theory is the direct application of SAE's core criterion, "structure over claim."

The Multi-Layer Diagnosis of Being Questioned

The Kingdom of Means' most common move with respect to Theorem Four is to forge the appearance of being questioned. It sets up mechanisms that look like questioning: parliaments, media, independent commissions of inquiry, and so on. But in actual operation these mechanisms may be no more than rubber stamps.

So Theorem Four needs a multi-layer diagnosis to be judged accurately:

Layer One: Does questioning exist?

The most basic layer. In the war-party's environment, is there any person, any institution, any channel carrying out questioning? If the answer is no — anyone who voices dissent is eliminated, every channel of questioning is closed — Theorem Four fails outright.

Layer Two: Can questioning reach the decision core?

Questioning that exists but stays at the margins does not amount to a real pass under Theorem Four. Questioning must be able to reach the decision core — the war-party's highest decision-makers, the core mechanism of decision, the concrete process of decision — otherwise it is only decoration.

Layer Three: Are questioners protected?

Are questioners suppressed, persecuted, or marginalized for questioning? If questioners are punished for the questioning itself — even if, for the moment, they can still question — Theorem Four also fails. For under such a structure questioning cannot be sustained, and will soon stop.

Layer Four: Do the results of questioning genuinely affect decisions?

Even if the first three layers are all satisfied, if the results of questioning carry no weight at all in decisions, then questioning is only performance. Questioning must genuinely change decisions — at least sometimes, at least in some cases, at least as a factor that decision-making cannot not take into account.

Only when all four layers pass does Theorem Four truly pass. If any one layer gives way, Theorem Four fails.

The Unforgeability of Being Questioned Has Two Time Scales

A deep structure must be stated here — forging openness to questioning may succeed on a short time scale, but on a long time scale it will certainly fail.

Short time scale: within a particular war, through tight control of information, performative mechanisms of questioning, covert persecution of questioners and similar means, a war-party can forge, before outside observers, the appearance of passing Theorem Four. Such forgery may get by for as long as the war lasts.

Long time scale: forged openness to questioning will necessarily give itself away on a long time scale. The reason is that genuine openness to questioning is an expression of the health of a society's subjectivity, and the health of subjectivity is, over the long run, directly reflected in the society's speed of development.

A society that does not allow genuine questioning has its internal subjects systematically suppressed. Suppressed subjects cannot contribute real creativity, judgment, or capacity for cooperation. Such a society necessarily falls behind, in speed of development, the societies that allow questioning. Over the long run, the gap accumulates exponentially.

So a war-party that forges Theorem Four, even if at some particular moment of the war it seems to have passed the criterion, will inevitably fall behind the subjects that genuinely pass Theorem Four in speed of development, on a time scale of years, decades, generations after the war. The gap at the level of development exposes the truth at the level of structure.

This is the other side of what §11 said — "war is not the home-ground of the Kingdom of Ends; development is" — the ultimate unforgeability of Theorem Four shows itself at the level of development. Forgery over a short span can get by; on a long time scale, forgery is exposed completely.

Where Questioners Come From

Questioners come from the four sources listed in Theorem Four's statement:

One's own ranks — including the war-party itself and the dissenters within its own side. Do the war-party's decision-makers question themselves? This is the innermost questioning. Does the war-party's society allow voices that differ from the mainstream to exist?

The other side's ranks — in the other side's society, too, there may be people questioning our conduct of the war (for example, the doubts and testimony that the other side's civilians raise about our conduct of the war).

Third parties — international institutions, neutral observers, humanitarian organizations, scholars, journalists: may they challenge the war-party's decisions without being treated as enemies?

Future history — is the war-party willing to accept future history's scrutiny of this war? Does it take the historical record into account when it decides?

The four sources form a complete diagnostic matrix. A qualified war-party allows questioning from all four sources.

Azeroth Example: Questioning Allowed

The Horde in Thrall's early period as Warchief is a demonstration of allowing questioning.

In that period, the Horde's major decisions were usually discussed in the council of chieftains (a council made up of the leaders of the various races). Some well-known examples: the tauren chieftain Cairne Bloodhoof raised objections to some of Thrall's policies, and these objections were genuinely discussed in council; the Darkspear troll leader Vol'jin spoke openly when he disagreed with some of Thrall's actions, and was not suppressed; Thrall himself, in a number of decisions, actually changed course in response to dissent from the council.

This is a near-complete demonstration of Theorem Four:

Layer One: questioning exists.

Layer Two: questioning reaches Thrall himself, that is, the decision core.

Layer Three: the questioners (Cairne, Vol'jin, and others) are not punished for questioning.

Layer Four: questioning sometimes genuinely affects decisions — Thrall would adjust his thinking in light of the council's views.

All four layers are satisfied. The Horde of this period is qualified under Theorem Four.

Azeroth Example: Questioning Not Allowed

The Horde in Garrosh's period as Warchief is the typical case of Theorem Four's reverse side.

After Garrosh took power, the decision-making structure within the Horde underwent a structural change:

Layer One (does questioning exist): Malkorok (a Blackrock orc whom Garrosh brought in for the purpose, head of the Kor'kron, Garrosh's personal bodyguard and chief adviser) was assigned to eliminate questioning systematically. In his own words: "questioning the Warchief is questioning the Horde." Under this system, dissent within the Horde was actively suppressed.

Layer Two (can it reach the decision core): the real decisions were made in secret — the true intent of the Theramore campaign was concealed from most of the Horde. Vol'jin and Baine (the new tauren chieftain, son of Cairne), as formal members of the council of chieftains, could voice dissent in council, but decisions were not made in council: questioning reached Garrosh in form, and was hollowed out in substance. This concealment from one's own side is precisely what §12 calls gui.

Layer Three (are questioners protected): Garrosh sent the Kor'kron to assassinate Vol'jin — the questioner became a target for murder. Vol'jin survived the attempt (he was attacked in Pandaria itself, and Chen Stormstout brought him to the Shado-Pan Monastery to recover), but the attempt on his life is in itself a direct violation of Theorem Four.

Layer Four (does questioning affect decisions): from the Divine Bell to the excavation of the Vale of Eternal Blossoms, not one of Garrosh's major decisions was adjusted because of dissent within the Horde.

All four layers fail.

The structural consequences of this complete violation of Theorem Four showed themselves at once — after surviving, Vol'jin launched the Darkspear Rebellion, which became the direct force that overthrew Garrosh. The structure of not allowing questioning itself became the direct fuse of the uprising.

This is Theorem Four's deep structure — a war-party not open to questioning contains, within its operating structure, its own collapse mechanism. For the subjectivity it suppresses does not disappear (SAE axiom); it can only return as resistance, uprising, internal collapse.

Suppressed questioning has, in each case, its own form of return: internal dissent that cannot be expressed becomes internal uprising; external challenges that cannot be engaged become structural alienation; future questioning that is preempted becomes retrospective condemnation, severe enough to destroy the war-party's historical legitimacy.

Garrosh used gui at Theramore (see Layer Two above; for the definition of gui, see §12), let no layer of the expansion at Theramore be honestly put to Theorem Two (§14), lost direction in Pandaria (§15), and suppressed questioning internally (§16) — multiple violations, whose structural consequences accumulated within a short time into an irretrievable outcome. This is no coincidence — actions violating structural criteria mutually reinforce structurally, forming a cycle of accelerating collapse.

17. The Shared Grammar and Penetration Depth of the Four Theorems

Together, the four theorems constitute the normative body of this war theory. This section integrates them, discussing their structure as a whole.

Shared Grammar: "Cannot-Not"

All four theorems begin with cannot-not:

  • Cannot-Not-Starting
  • Cannot-Not-Expanding
  • Cannot-Not-Be-Directed-Toward-Ending
  • Cannot-Not-Be-Open-to-Questioning

This is not wordplay; it is the embodiment of principled unity. "Cannot-not" as shared grammar carries a structural function — stripping the grammatical subject of its sense of power.

The Kingdom of Means, when handling war, uses typical grammars: "I decide," "I permit," "I judge," "I have the right." These grammars place the war-party in the position of sovereign, granting it the power to choose how to fight this war, when to end it, whether to accept questioning.

SAE replaces these sovereign grammars with "cannot-not." The meaning is this — the war-party is not "choosing" to open war; it is forced by structure into a corner, and cannot not open it; it is not "permitting" questioning; it has no position of non-questioning to stand on. The sovereign's arrogance is directly stripped away by this grammar.

This grammatical discipline is not a rhetorical preference; it is the physical outer shell of principle. Once the war-party is granted a "sovereign position of permission" at the grammatical level, a door is structurally opened for the war-party to slide toward the Kingdom of Means. SAE's war theory uses the hardest grammar to weld this door shut.

The Penetration Depth of "Cannot-Not"

The four theorems share the "cannot-not" grammar, but the object that "cannot-not" constrains differs in each, forming a sequence of increasingly deep penetration:

Theorem One (opening): constrains the initiation of action. Here "cannot-not" limits the war-party's first action — whether to begin the war.

Theorem Two (expansion): constrains the continuation and escalation of action. Here "cannot-not" limits each of the war-party's choices of action while the war is under way.

Theorem Three (direction toward ending): constrains the vector of action. Here "cannot-not" limits the direction of the war-party's actions — every action must be directed toward ending.

Theorem Four (being questioned): constrains the war-party's own position. Here "cannot-not" does not constrain any particular action; it constrains the war-party's state as a subject — it cannot not stand in the position of being questioned, and has no position of non-questioning to stand on.

From initiation to continuation to vector to position — penetration deepens layer by layer. Theorem Four penetrates deepest — it no longer constrains any action; it constrains the very form of the war-party's existence as a subject. Under Theorem Four the war-party loses the position of "permitting subject" — it is not the one who permits questioning; it is the one who bears it.

This structure of deepening layers makes the four theorems not parallel but concentric-ring constraints — each theorem constrains the war-party at a deeper layer; the outermost ring constrains concrete actions, the innermost constrains the very form of the subject's existence.

Each ring asks one question:

  • The outermost ring — Theorem One — asks: Should this war be opened at all?
  • The second ring — Theorem Two — asks: Should this expansion happen at all?
  • The third ring — Theorem Three — asks: Is this action moving toward ending?
  • The innermost ring — Theorem Four — asks: Is this war-party the kind of subject that can be held to the questions above?

The Mutual Reinforcement of the Four Theorems

One last structural observation — the four theorems are not independent of one another; they reinforce one another.

A subject that opens war in a qualified way under Theorem One will usually also judge expansion strictly under Theorem Two, stay directed toward ending under Theorem Three, and allow questioning under Theorem Four. This is no coincidence — these behaviors all come from the same set of structural judgments with one principled source (letting war, as a means, serve cultivation).

Conversely, a subject that violates one of the theorems will usually violate the others as well. Garrosh's case has already shown this — violation in gui (§12, §16), violation in expansion never honestly tested (§14), violation in losing the direction toward ending (§15), violation in suppressing questioning (§16). These violations are not independent events; they are multiple symptoms of one and the same structural tendency — all different faces of the single process by which war slides from protector of cultivation to interrupter of cultivation.

Why this is usually so can be seen from how the violations draw one another in. A war-party that violates Theorem Two (unqualified expansion) is pushed to violate Theorem Three — its objectives have drifted, and it is no longer directed toward the original ending; and it is pushed to violate Theorem Four — when questioned, it cannot account for the drift, so it must suppress questioning. A war-party that violates Theorem One (opening war without a genuine cannot-not) is pushed to violate Theorem Four — when questioned about where its cannot-not lies, it cannot produce one, so it must silence the questioning. So a war-party that has violated any of the first three will usually, in the end, be forced to violate Theorem Four to cover up; and the violation of Theorem Four is visible from outside.

This mutual reinforcement means that recognizing whether a war is qualified usually does not require a complete judgment under all four theorems; looking at one or two of them usually reveals the whole picture. Theorem Four, as the externally observable diagnostic, is especially important — looking at Theorem Four usually lets one judge quickly the rough state of the other three. But this is a shortcut to recognition, not the condition of qualification — qualification requires all four to hold.

The four theorems are one. Structurally, together they define "war that serves cultivation" — under what conditions war, as a means, is still working for cultivation, and under what conditions it has already slid toward interrupting cultivation. The four theorems are not "norms of war"; they are the protective mechanism of cultivation in the situation of war.

The Middle Volume ends here. The Lower Volume takes up the three ending forms of war — victory, failure, equilibrium — and how cultivation recovers in each.

Lower Volume · Three Ending Forms

Once a war has been opened and is conducted in a qualified way, it must eventually end. Theorem Three says ending is war's direction. The Lower Volume handles the three actual ending forms of war — victory, failure, equilibrium — and how cultivation recovers in each.

These three forms are not chosen by the war-party. They are the outcomes of how the two sides' structural positions interact over the course of the war. What the war-party can choose is — within the ending form it finds itself in, how to let cultivation recover. This is the core question of the Lower Volume.

The four sections of the Lower Volume each carry one form or the integrative work —

§18 Victory (the situation of 15DD's resistance succeeding) — How not to slide back into 14DD after resistance succeeds. Core work includes dismantling wartime structures, the surrogate-liberation firewall, and not colonizing, but maintained observation.

§19 Failure (the situation of 15DD's resistance failing) — How to preserve the carriers of cultivation after resistance fails. Core work includes anti-yu-sui as an ontological commandment, and three qualified responses (trade interest for time, flee, fragmenting-and-dispersing). Fragmenting-and-dispersing runs in two directions: inward fragmentation (cultivation passing into micro-forms) and outward connection (seeking allies of the same structure).

§20 Equilibrium (the situation where neither side can decide the outcome) — Ceasefire, returning to fa-mou and fa-jiao, preventing the toxic mirror-assimilation of long-term equilibrium.

§21 The Shared Structure of the Three Ending Forms — Victory, failure, and equilibrium differ on the surface, but under SAE they point to the same thing: letting cultivation truly return to home-ground after the war.

The three forms differ greatly on the surface, but together they point to one thing — letting war, this extreme-state tool, truly complete its work, letting cultivation become home-ground again after the war. If the war ends but cultivation does not recover, then war as a means has not truly completed its work, and the cost of the war has been paid in vain.

The four sections follow.

18. Victory (the Situation of 15DD's Resistance Succeeding)

Victory is the first ending form of war — the 15DD party's resistance succeeds; the 14DD party's expansion is blocked or dissolved.

The SAE definition of victory has to be laid down with great care, because the victorious party faces a structural temptation — sliding, in the course of winning, toward the very structure it was resisting. The organizational machinery, wartime mobilization, centralized decision-making and internal discipline built up in resisting 14DD are structurally similar to the systematized way 14DD operates. Once resistance succeeds, the victorious party feels an internal pull to harden these wartime structures into a new systematized oppression — if it is not vigilant, it may, having defeated 14DD, become a new 14DD itself.

So this section must define what counts as a qualified victory and, above all, establish the concrete criterion for not sliding back into 14DD after resistance succeeds.

The SAE Definition of Victory

Victory: the "cannot-not" that existed at the opening of war is dissolved through the war; at the moment of that dissolution, the other side still exists as a cultivation-subject; and the victorious party itself has not slid toward new 14DD systemic purpose in the process.

This definition has three necessary conditions — "the cannot-not is dissolved," "the other side still exists as a cultivation-subject," and "the victorious party itself has not slid toward new 14DD." None can be missing.

If "the cannot-not is dissolved" but "the other side no longer exists as a cultivation-subject" (it has been eliminated, completely transformed, or disbanded), this is not victory — it is an ending form that has interrupted the other side's cultivation. However thorough a victory it looks militarily, under SAE it is no longer victory; it is a deformed form that needs separate discussion (taken up below).

If "the other side still exists as a cultivation-subject" but "the cannot-not has not been dissolved" (the war has not reached its objective; cultivation has not regained the conditions for recovery), this is not victory either — it is failure or equilibrium.

If the first two hold but the victorious party has, in the course of the war, itself upgraded into a new 14DD system (built its own systematized structure of oppression, its own expansionary purpose, its own mechanisms of internal instrumentalization), this too is not victory under SAE — it is the failure of a resisting party that has slid toward the very structure it resisted. Under SAE such a "victory" is in fact a loss for cultivation as a whole, because the world now has one fewer 15DD resister and one more 14DD oppressor.

Only when all three conditions hold at once is there victory under SAE — 15DD resistance succeeds, the other side's cultivation-carrier is preserved, and the victorious party itself has not slid toward new 14DD.

The Difference from Kingdom-of-Means Victory

The Kingdom-of-Means definition of victory is usually "one side wins outright" — the other surrenders, the other is defeated, the other accepts one's terms. The core of this definition is about the other side's state — the other lost, so I won.

The core of the SAE definition of victory is about the state of cultivation as a whole — cultivation on both sides can continue (the other side is preserved as a cultivation-subject), the cultivation the victorious party set out to protect has regained its conditions, and the victorious party's own cultivation has not been interrupted along the way by the wartime structures it built.

While the war is being fought, the two definitions may look the same — under both, the war-party works to stop the other's oppression, to disable the enemy's armed forces, to dissolve the threat. But the two definitions diverge immediately at the post-war stage.

The Kingdom-of-Means view of victory inclines the war-party, after the war, naturally toward consolidating the fruits of victory, preventing the other's resurgence, transforming the other to eliminate future threats, and hardening wartime structures into peacetime dominance structures. The SAE view of victory puts the war-party, after the war, immediately in front of a structural problem — the "cannot-not" has been dissolved; every action the victorious party now takes must pass the criterion afresh. Every post-war action is a new action, requiring a new cannot-not to be legitimate. More fundamentally — the wartime structures of resistance must be actively dismantled after the war; the organizational machinery of resistance must not be allowed to become the machinery of peacetime oppression.

The Greatest Post-Victory Temptation

The victorious party's greatest temptation is — transforming wartime oppressive structures into peacetime dominance structures.

During the war, in order to conduct the war, the victorious party cannot not build certain oppressive structures: a centralized command system, temporary denial of the enemy's subjectivity, wartime emergency powers, temporary restrictions on dissent. These structures may be qualified during the war (through Theorem Two's expansion criterion, they may satisfy "cannot-not" in specific wartime situations).

Post-war, the legitimacy of these structures immediately vanishes — the war has ended, the wartime rationales no longer exist. But these structures still physically exist: the command system has not automatically dissolved, emergency powers have not automatically been handed back, restrictions on dissent have not automatically been lifted. The victorious party faces a structural choice — actively dismantle these wartime structures, or let them persist as peacetime dominance structures?

If it chooses persistence, that is the transformation of wartime oppressive structures into permanent dominance structures. Once this step is complete, the victorious party has, after the war, turned itself into a new machine of 14DD systemic purpose — oppressing its own people (through the power machinery built in wartime), and possibly the people of the defeated party as well (under the guise of "managing the post-war situation"). This is precisely the structural slide established in §5 — after 15DD's resistance succeeds, if the organizational machinery of resistance is not dismantled, it becomes a new 14DD systemic purpose, carrying on the very structure it once resisted.

The most common path by which a victorious party slides toward the Kingdom of Means after the war is this "permanentization of wartime structures."

The Victorious Party's Obligations

Under SAE the victorious party has two clear structural obligations:

Obligation One — Actively dismantle wartime structures. All the temporary structures built in wartime — emergency powers, mechanisms of oppression, mobilization systems, wartime narrative — must be actively dismantled after the war ends. Not "let them naturally fade"; not "keep them in case." Active dismantling: command systems restructured to peacetime norms, emergency powers formally relinquished, restrictions on dissent formally lifted, wartime mobilization machinery formally demobilized. The speed and thoroughness of the dismantling directly determine whether the victorious party is still within the Kingdom of Ends.

Obligation Two — Not substitute for the defeated party's reorganization of subjectivity. Stated in full: the victorious party must exit the reorganization space of the defeated party's subjectivity. Winning does not confer a right to transform the defeated party. How the defeated party reorganizes its own society, chooses its own leaders and restores its own subjectivity is the defeated party's own affair. A victorious party that crosses the boundary of "dissolving the threat" to manage the defeated party's internal affairs — even in the name of "reconstruction," "humanitarian aid" or "preventing instability" — is exercising the 15DD right of transformation in the defeated party's place. This violates Theorem Three's Methods-Constraint Bridge (it seals off the defeated party's possibility of reorganizing itself as a subject).

These two obligations together form the surrogate-liberation firewall.

The Surrogate-Liberation Firewall

The most deceptive temptation the victorious party faces after the war is — liberating the other side on its behalf.

During the war, if the victorious party is on the Kingdom-of-Ends side and the defeated party on the Kingdom-of-Means side, winning has in itself given the subjects oppressed within the defeated party some chance of liberation. At this point the victorious party very easily falls into a line of thought — since we have already won, we should "help" the liberated within the defeated party build their new society, new order, new institutions.

On the surface, this thinking looks benevolent. In substance it is surrogate-liberation — the victorious party deciding what the defeated party should become. This transfers the liberated from one form of 14DD systemic-purpose oppression (the defeated party's original Kingdom-of-Means structure) to another form of 14DD systemic-purpose oppression (the transformation structure imposed by the victorious party in the name of aid). The liberated's subjectivity has not truly been released; it has only changed the structure of oppression.

The core of the surrogate-liberation firewall is — the victorious party shall not decide on the defeated party's direction of subjectivity-development on their behalf. Not substituting for the defeated party's value-ceiling, not substituting for their narrative direction, not substituting for their future form. Post-threat-dissolution reconstruction must be handed back quickly to the defeated party's internal genuine subjects.

The surrogate-liberation firewall in the SAE overall framework is the natural extension of Note 9's directionality constraint ("I don't collect") in the post-war situation — Note 9 establishes the principle that the direction of cultivation is decided by the subject itself, and that the external cannot decide the subject's cultivation-direction on its behalf; the surrogate-liberation firewall applies this same principle to the post-war field — even when the victorious party is temporarily in an overwhelming position, it cannot decide the defeated party's cultivation-direction on its behalf. The directionality constraint appears in wartime as "not deciding for the other," and in post-war as "not reconstructing for the defeated party"; the principle is the same.

Not Colonizing, But Maintained Observation

The firewall does not mean absolute non-intervention. Absolute non-intervention is the opposite extreme, and another error.

If, after the victorious party withdraws, new 14DD systemic-purpose expansion/oppression immediately emerges within the defeated party (new extremists, new warlords, new Kingdom-of-Means forces carrying out slaughter against the defeated party's internal subjects), must the victorious party hold to non-intervention?

The answer is — no. This constitutes a new "cannot-not" situation. This situation is not a continuation of the previous war; it is an independent application of Theorem One. Whether the victorious party re-intervenes is re-evaluated under the fresh criterion. Every condition of this new round of Theorem One must still be passed in full: the victorious party's own first face must hold, and the slaughter within the other side, as a second face, cannot launch a re-intervention on its own (§13). To say that it "constitutes a new cannot-not situation" is to say that it calls for a fresh judgment under Theorem One, not that the cannot-not already holds.

So the correct expression of the firewall is not "the victorious party absolutely does not intervene," but — the victorious party does not colonize, but maintains observation.

Not colonizing holds the firewall — not substituting for the defeated party's value-ceiling, not substituting for its narrative direction, not substituting for its direction of subjectivity-development.

Maintained observation holds open Theorem One's re-evaluation channel — if new 14DD systemic-purpose expansion/oppression emerges, recognize it, evaluate it, and judge against Theorem One whether to re-intervene.

These two together: not colonizing, but maintained observation.

The concrete form — whether the victorious party stations troops near the defeated party, how large the deployment, under what rules, for how long — these are all questions of concrete form, judged by military and political leaders according to specific situations. Philosophy gives direction, not quantitative prescription. SAE war theory gives only the direction "not colonizing, but maintained observation"; the concrete form is not within this treatise's object.

Azeroth Example: The Paradigm of Victory

The Alliance's conduct after the Siege of Orgrimmar is a complete demonstration of the victorious party's obligations.

The structure at the war's end — Garrosh has been captured, his authority has collapsed, his oppression of the Horde's other races is gone, and his military threat to the Alliance has been dissolved. The "cannot-not" has been dissolved.

What Alliance High King Varian Wrynn did:

First, dismantle wartime structures: once the siege was over, the Alliance's wartime command system was immediately returned to its peacetime state. No new occupation system was set up; no wartime power structure was retained.

Second, exit the reorganization space of the defeated party's subjectivity: Alliance forces withdrew from Orgrimmar and took no part in choosing the Horde's new leadership. The new Warchief was chosen by the Horde itself (Vol'jin succeeded).

Third, the surrogate-liberation firewall: the Alliance did not try to "help" the Horde rebuild — it stationed no representatives, set up no supervisory bodies, and did not intervene in the Horde's internal politics. The Horde was reorganized entirely by the Horde.

Fourth, maintained observation: Varian's famous words to the new Warchief — "The Horde has committed heinous crimes, Vol'jin. But some among you fought against Garrosh's tyranny. For that, I am willing to end this bloodshed. But know this… If your Horde fails to uphold honor, as Garrosh did… We will end you." — are the demonstration of "maintained observation." The Alliance did not cut off relations once it had left; it kept a certain position of observation — if new 14DD systemic-purpose expansion/oppression were to arise again within the Horde (including failing to uphold honor, as Garrosh did), the Alliance would re-evaluate under Theorem One. But this observation is not continued intervention.

This is the complete demonstration of the victorious party's obligations. Not because Varian is morally noble (SAE does not make moral judgments), but because Varian's actions structurally passed every criterion for the victorious party.

Azeroth Example: The Victorious Party Sliding Toward the Kingdom of Means

The Alliance's handling of orc prisoners after the Second War is the typical case of a victorious party violating Theorem Three.

At the end of the Second War, the Alliance had defeated the orcish Horde militarily and completely. The Horde's main force was shattered; most orcs had been captured or scattered. The Alliance now faced a choice — what to do with these prisoners?

The Alliance chose internment camps. The "Alliance Internment Act" concentrated large numbers of orcs in dedicated camps and held them there. It came out of a debate within the Alliance after the Second War over what to do with the captives: Terenas argued for internment, Trollbane and Greymane for execution, and they settled on the compromise of life imprisonment — on its face a reasonable post-war security measure.

Under SAE, however, the structural consequences of this choice were disastrous:

First, it crossed the boundary of "dissolving the threat." The orcs' military power had been shattered; the main threat had been dissolved. Continuing to hold the orcs as a whole went beyond the scope of "directed toward ending" under Theorem Three — this was not dissolving a "cannot-not"; it was using a wartime oppressive structure to maintain permanent control over the defeated party.

Second, it decided the orcs' future for them. The orcs were not allowed to reorganize as subjects. The internment system amounted to denying the orcs' continued existence as subjects, treating them as a population to be "managed."

Third, it created the soil for the next war. In the camps, under the dual effects of blood-pact withdrawal (§12), the orcs sank into long lethargy. But subjectivity cannot be eliminated (an SAE axiom) — suppressed subjectivity necessarily returns. Thrall's uprising is the concrete form of that return.

The complete collapse of the surrogate-liberation firewall. The Alliance's actions after the Second War looked militarily like "the victor's reasonable management"; under SAE they are the typical case of a victorious party sliding toward the Kingdom of Means.

A telling contrast is an extreme case — Aedelas Blackmoore had already taken the infant orc Thrall from beside his slain parents during the First War; after the orcs' defeat in the Second War, while running the camps that held the orcs, he raised Thrall in his own castle, Durnholde Keep, as a slave gladiator. This pushes "managing the defeated" to its extreme — an individual of the defeated party taken from his people and shaped to the victor's needs. What Blackmoore did looked like "reasonable use of resources" in his time; under SAE it is a complete demonstration of re-oppressing the liberated (Thrall, as a son of the orcs, could have returned to the Horde after the war).

The true structure of surrogate-liberation — using the liberated as tools once more.

The deep lesson of this example is — if the victorious party does not actively dismantle wartime structures, does not actively exit the reorganization space of the defeated party's subjectivity, it does not merely make an error; it structurally re-creates the conditions that lead to the next war.

19. Failure (the Situation of 15DD's Resistance Failing)

Failure is the second ending form of war — the 15DD party's resistance fails, the 14DD party's expansion succeeds.

By rights, this situation "should not occur." §11 established that civilizations with deeper cultivation lead in development speed, and that the Kingdom of Ends prevails over the long run with high probability. But "high probability" is not "necessarily." Remainders always exist — 14DD systemic purpose's expansion overpowering 15DD resistance at specific historical junctures has happened again and again. A 15DD party with deeper cultivation may, at some specific historical moment, be unable to defeat a cruder but more violent, more expansionist 14DD opponent. This is a structural reality SAE does not evade.

What this section handles is — after the 15DD party's resistance fails, how to preserve the cultivation-carriers that have already been accumulated.

This section carries two pieces of work — first, laying down anti-yu-sui as a hard-core claim of SAE war theory addressed to the history of human war thought; second, giving the three qualified responses after resistance fails (trading interest for time, fleeing, fragmenting-and-dispersing).

The response after resistance fails is not merely an individual subject's choice — it also determines whether a 15DD civilization, after being conquered by 14DD, can let its cultivation persist in dispersed form through long stretches of time, until the moment comes again. This is the cultivation work that 15DD can still do in the deepest adversity.

The SAE Definition of Failure

Failure: the concrete carrier of cultivation fails to hold, but cultivation itself does not terminate.

Both parts of this definition matter.

"The concrete carrier fails to hold" — the specific position, the specific structure, the specific form of cultivation that one set out to defend when the war was opened was not held in the war. This is the objective fact of failure in military and structural terms. The main position is lost; the capital falls; the original organization dissolves; the original way of life cannot go on — these are all failures of the concrete carrier.

"Cultivation itself does not terminate" — the possibility of cultivation continuing to unfold in subjects remains. Not in its pre-war form, not on that main position, but cultivation can still continue in subjects. It may continue in exile, in dispersion, in memory, in the next generation, with the support of external allies. So long as cultivation itself has not terminated, it is still a qualified failure.

Taken together, the two parts mean — failure is the failure of a concrete form of cultivation, not the disappearance of cultivation itself. After failure, cultivation is still cultivation, only continuing in new, reduced, transferred, ongoing forms.

Failure Does Not Equal SAE Losing

SAE's true losing, as said earlier, is the war-party interrupting its own civilization's cultivation in the course of the war.

Failure and true losing are two different things.

A subject can fail completely in military terms and yet keep its cultivation continuing throughout the failure and after it — under SAE, this subject has not lost. It has only failed. Its cultivation has been preserved.

A subject can win victory after victory in military terms and yet interrupt its own cultivation in the course of winning — under SAE, this subject has lost. This holds even if it has gained territory, defeated its adversary, and taken the dominant position.

The axes of the two judgments are entirely different — failure is a 14DD-level matter (a concrete military/structural outcome); true losing is a cultivation-level matter (whether cultivation has been interrupted).

This distinction is crucial to the failing party. Because it tells the failing party — you can fail without losing. In a situation of failure, there are still things you can do to keep cultivation continuing. The concrete form of the failure may be beyond your control (the balance of military strength, external aid, chance factors, and so on), but whether you let cultivation continue through the failure lies in your own judgment and action.

Anti-Yu-sui: The Principal Difference Between SAE War Theory and 14DD War Theory

Before unfolding the failing party's concrete responses, one hard-core claim of SAE war theory must first be laid down — anti-yu-sui.

This is one of the principal differences between SAE war theory and 14DD war theory. Nearly all war theories within the 14DD framework affirm yu-sui in some situation — when defeat is beyond retrieval, when humiliation is unacceptable, yu-sui is held to be the honorable choice. Expressions like "better to break as jade than be preserved as tile" recur in various forms across 14DD civilizations. Yu-sui in 14DD narratives carries tragic grandeur, glory, the dignity of "at least we did not submit."

SAE war theory explicitly opposes this. Yu-sui is structurally unqualified under SAE. In no situation is yu-sui a qualified failure-form.

Let me unfold the structural reasons for this opposition.

The SAE Axiom-Layer Opposition to Yu-sui

The SAE axioms say — subjectivity cannot be eliminated; cultivation cannot not continue.

What is the structure of yu-sui? It is the subject actively choosing to let the cultivation carrier disappear. A civilization facing defeat decides "we all fight to the death; no one remains" — this is the subject actively demanding its own disappearance.

This directly conflicts with the SAE axioms. The SAE axioms say cultivation cannot not continue; yu-sui says cultivation actively terminates in this subject. This is not termination imposed from outside but termination chosen by the subject itself. Yu-sui is the subject's active abandonment of the SAE axioms.

Yu-sui makes the carrier disappear. When the carrier disappears, cultivation terminates in this subject. The termination of cultivation is not only this subject's loss — it is the loss of every other subject in a cultivation-relationship with it. The cultivation in a subject carries its history of mutual-chiseling with other subjects, the memory of growing together, the possible directions of future growth. The disappearance of the carrier means that all of these disappear with it.

Cultivation Needs Mutual-Chiseling; Yu-sui Eliminates the Mutual-Chiseling Partner

§5 established — cultivation needs mutual-chiseling. Mutual-chiseling is always needed. A subject without mutual-chiseling shrinks into self-enclosure. Cultivation is not the affair of a single subject; it is the affair of all subjects together.

Yu-sui makes a potential mutual-chiseling partner disappear forever. Even if the other side is the present enemy, over the long run it may be a mutual-chiseling partner — a failed civilization, if preserved, may have descendants who in the future become part of a mutual-chiseling relationship with the descendants of today's victor. Yu-sui severs this possibility.

From the perspective of cultivation, yu-sui is a loss to everyone — to the one who chooses yu-sui (its cultivation terminates), to that party's adversary (a mutual-chiseling partner is lost), and to the whole civilizational ecology (a node of cultivation is permanently lost).

Yu-sui Is Structurally Impossible Under SAE Axioms

There is a deeper layer — yu-sui as such is structurally impossible under the SAE axioms.

The SAE axioms say subjectivity cannot be eliminated. But "subjectivity cannot be eliminated" is not a guarantee — it is a structural fact. Even if the carrier undergoes yu-sui, subjectivity returns in other forms: in surviving descendants, in historical memory, in what witnesses carry forward, in the structural shadow of the adversary, in the culture, language, traditions, and influence left behind by those who were shattered.

So the state of "complete disappearance" that yu-sui pursues does not exist under SAE — it only appears to exist. At the very moment the subject undergoing yu-sui believes it has completed its disappearance, subjectivity has already begun to return in new forms. The strength of the return is proportional to the resoluteness of the yu-sui — the more thorough the yu-sui, the greater the force of return (because the trauma, memory, and cultural burden left behind run deeper).

Pursuing yu-sui is a structurally impossible goal. The subject undergoing yu-sui believes it has obtained a "pure ending"; in fact it has only made cultivation return in more painful, more distorted, more delayed ways. The cost of yu-sui is real (the disappearance of one generation or several), but what yu-sui aims to achieve (complete disappearance) is false.

Anti-Yu-sui Is Not SAE's Invention

A posture must be made clear here.

SAE is not the first to oppose yu-sui — the structural intuition against yu-sui has appeared again and again in human history, even though this intuition has never been systematized into a principle.

That this intuition keeps appearing is no accident. It is because cultivation, as a structural need of subjectivity, is trans-temporal and trans-cultural. Subjects of any era and any culture, once they face the tension between "cultivation" and "the carrier disappearing," may sense the structural intuition that "yu-sui is not right" — because this intuition comes from the universality of cultivation itself. The universality of cultivation does not depend on any particular culture, any particular era, or the establishment of any particular philosophical language. It is a structural fact of subjectivity, the same in every subject.

So in any era, some subject or civilization may, in some situation, sense that "yu-sui is not right". In one era this sense may be voiced in concrete language such as "life matters," "keep the green hills and there will be firewood," "endure humiliation to bear the burden," "preserve a life that can still be of use"; in another era, in other language. Each era's form of expression differs, and they do not easily translate into one another. But structurally they point to the same thing — a subject should not actively let its cultivation carrier disappear.

What SAE war theory does is not to invent anti-yu-sui; it is to give anti-yu-sui a complete structural account for the first time. SAE says — yu-sui is not right because it makes cultivation terminate in that carrier, which violates the structural requirement of cultivation as the foundation of the principles. SAE makes statable a judgment that has long been present in the structure of cultivation but could never be completely said.

This is the posture of cultivation — not cleverer than human history, only finding, for a structural need that cultivation itself has always had, a principled language that can be passed on across eras.

Yu-sui Only Stably Becomes a Visible Option When Both Sides Remain in 14DD

A further structural observation is worth laying down — yu-sui only stably becomes a visible option when both sides are locked in 14DD language.

Why? Because yu-sui requires the two sides' 14DD languages to match —

The oppressed side is in 14DD — it understands its own situation through the language of "honor / victory-defeat / peer collision." In this language, as defeat closes in, yu-sui looks like the honorable choice.

The oppressing side is also in 14DD — it treats the other as an enemy (rather than as a subject that has not yet stood up). In this posture, it drives the other into a corner and leaves the other no room to continue as a subject.

Only when the two sides' 14DD languages match does yu-sui stably become an option visible to both sides.

If the oppressing side is genuinely 15DD — its structural goal is for the other to truly stand up as a subject (developed in §18) — it will not push the other to the edge of yu-sui. It will give the other room to recognize cultivation, will not demonize the other, will keep channels of communication open, will let the other see "what I want is for you to stand up, not for you to disappear." A genuine 15DD actively keeps the yu-sui situation from arising.

If the oppressed side is genuinely 15DD (the core situation of this section) — it will not choose yu-sui itself. A subject genuinely operating at 15DD has already recognized that cultivation is what must be protected; it knows yu-sui is the active abandonment of the SAE axioms. It will choose the three qualified responses laid down below — trading interest for time, fleeing, fragmenting-and-dispersing.

So the occurrence of yu-sui is itself a structural diagnosis — only when both sides are in 14DD does yu-sui become stably visible; only when at least one side is in 14DD can yu-sui occur at all. In any war where yu-sui occurs, at least one side is still in 14DD. Yu-sui is a product of 14DD, not a necessary product of war. In explicitly opposing yu-sui, SAE war theory explicitly requires every subject that enters war to evolve toward 15DD — or at the very least not to let itself fall back into 14DD.

The Degradation Risk of Subjects Evolving Toward 15DD Under Failure Pressure

But a dangerous situation must be specifically handled here — subjects in the process of evolving toward 15DD may, under the pressure of defeat, regress to 14DD.

A subject evolving from 14DD toward 15DD has already begun to recognize cultivation, has already felt the structural intuition that "yu-sui is not right," has already been moving toward 15DD in its organization, narrative, and internal structure. But evolution is not completed in one step — it is an ongoing, fragile process.

The extreme pressure of impending defeat can reverse this evolution. When a subject feels cornered between "either humiliation or yu-sui," it may regress to the 14DD framework — repackaging yu-sui in the 14DD language of "purity," "dignity," "not bowing to barbarism." This packaging is especially dangerous because it speaks in cultivation's own language — "our civilization is too precious to live on in humiliation."

This is the purism trap — demanding, in cultivation's name, the disappearance of cultivation's carrier. It sounds like a defense of cultivation; in substance it violates cultivation. A subject genuinely operating at 15DD can recognize this trap: when the carrier disappears, cultivation is cut off; there is no "purity" more important than the continuation of the carrier.

One important work of anti-yu-sui is to protect evolution from being interrupted — continually reminding subjects under the pressure of defeat that the continuation of their cultivation carrier matters more than any "purity."

This is also why anti-yu-sui must be laid down by SAE war theory head-on, as a hard-core claim, and repeatedly. Anti-yu-sui is not prepared for "mature 15DD subjects who would never choose yu-sui" — it is prepared for all subjects in the process of evolving, all subjects who may regress to 14DD under the pressure of defeat. It is a standing structural warning line.

Three Qualified Responses When 15DD Fails

With anti-yu-sui laid down, the positive question is — when the 15DD side faces defeat, what are the qualified responses?

According to the nature of the 14DD opponent, there are three sub-situations, each with its own qualified response.

Sub-situation One: The 14DD Opponent Wants Interest — Trade Interest for Time

The mildest situation. The opponent wants resources, territory, trade rights, taxes, a posture of submission. The opponent does not pursue the 15DD subject's disappearance; it only demands that the 15DD submit in some form.

The response is to trade interest for time. Accept some concessions at the level of interest (tribute, territorial cession, reparations, border concessions, nominal submission) while preserving one's wholeness as a cultivation-subject. What is ceded is the concrete form, not the cultivation carrier itself.

This response has appeared again and again in human history — weaker civilizations of deeper cultivation paying tribute to stronger barbarian conquerors, ceding interest in exchange for peace and time. Time is cultivation's ally — as time passes, the gap in cultivation between the two sides accumulates as a gap in development speed; within a few generations the conqueror is either absorbed by cultivation itself (the pattern of "the conquerors conquered by the civilization they conquered") or declines from within, and the relation of conquest dissolves of its own accord.

The key to trading interest for time is distinguishing clearly what can be ceded from what cannot:

Can be ceded: concrete interests, temporary dignity, nominal allegiance, partial autonomy, economic concessions.

Cannot be ceded: the cultivation carrier itself (culture, language, spiritual tradition, educational system, the operation of subjectivity in daily life). Once these begin to be replaced, it is no longer trading interest for time; it is gradually accepting colonization.

Sub-situation Two: The 14DD Opponent Wants Colonization/Slaughter and Flight Is Possible — Flee

A situation of medium severity. The opponent is not content with interest; it demands that the 15DD disappear as a subject — either by accepting replacement by the opponent's 14DD systemic purpose (colonization), or by physical elimination (slaughter). But the 15DD still has the strength and the space to withdraw.

The response is to flee. Not to flee in shame — but to recognize clearly that the present concrete form cannot be maintained, and to choose actively to defer to a future in which rebuilding is possible.

The structural meaning of flight: the main force withdraws, core personnel are transferred, important resources are protected, and the carriers of cultural tradition (books, crafts, knowledge, spiritual inheritance) are moved first to safe places. Flight accepts a loss of time (the continuity of history, perhaps several generations of displacement) in order to preserve the possibility that cultivation itself will continue.

This response requires clear judgment from the leadership — choosing to withdraw while it still looks as though a fight could be made often preserves cultivation better than withdrawing in desperate straits. A subject that flees early can carry out its core resources in good order; a subject that flees in desperation can often carry out only a small number of survivors.

Sub-situation Three: The 14DD Opponent Wants Colonization/Slaughter and Flight Is Impossible — Fragmenting-and-Dispersing

The most severe situation. The opponent demands the 15DD's disappearance as a subject, and the 15DD has no space for an overall withdrawal (geographic encirclement, surrounded by every neighboring subject, nowhere to go).

The response is fragmenting-and-dispersing.

This response has two directions —

One, inward fragmentation

The civilization's overall organization dissolves, but cultivation does not disappear. Cultivation shifts from macro, organized, visible forms into micro, individual, familial, invisible forms. Every individual, every small group, every family, every person who carries cultural transmission preserves itself separately, dispersing to every corner, to every position in time and space, each carrying seeds of cultivation to distant places.

The deep mechanism of inward fragmentation — cultivation goes underground, into families, into individual hearts. On the surface the civilization has disappeared; in substance cultivation has passed into micro-forms that are extremely hard to destroy. Generations later, cultivation may re-emerge in a new form, different from the original civilizational form, yet it is the same cultivation continuing on a new carrier.

Occupiers can destroy a government, a capital, an army, but they find it hard to completely destroy millions of dispersed individual hearts. So long as cultivation persists at the level of individuals and families, the occupation itself cannot completely succeed. Every individual who under occupation still holds to their own culture, their own language, their own values is a seed of cultivation that is growing. Every secretly circulated text, every oral story handed down the generations, every traditional festival still cherished under oppression is a manifestation of cultivation in dispersed form.

The concrete actions under occupation — underground resistance, secret education, the covert continuation of cultural transmission, the not-truly-accepting of the occupier's 14DD systemic purpose (outward compliance while inwardly preserving subjectivity), continued waiting for the future — these are all concrete forms of inward fragmentation.

Two, outward connection

Fragmenting-and-dispersing is not only dispersal inward — it also includes seeking outward for allies of the same structure.

This layer matters. A completely conquered civilization, if it can find allies within a larger cultivation-network, turns its fragmenting-and-dispersing from a merely passive survival strategy into actively weaving its own cultivation into a trans-subject cultivation-network. A single subject may fail, but cultivation as a trans-subject phenomenon remains. The threat external allies face may be the very threat one faces oneself — resisting the expansion of the same Kingdom of Means — and this gives the allies' aid a structural anchor.

Four points on seeking allies outward —

One, find allies of the same structure. An ally must itself be a subject operating at 15DD (or evolving toward 15DD). If one allies with another party that has slid into 14DD ("the enemy of my enemy is my friend"), then after the alliance the ally will act in 14DD ways (slaughter, surrogate-liberation, and so on), and the cultivation that fragmenting-and-dispersing preserved is destroyed by the ally itself. An alliance must hold to structural kinship in cultivation, not merely to a short-term alignment of interests.

Two, the ally must protect subjectivity, not liberate on the failed party's behalf. The ally's aid must be directed toward "letting the failed party stand up again as a subject," not toward "helping the failed party get liberated and then making it our appendage." This structurally echoes §18's surrogate-liberation firewall — trans-subject alliance relations must hold the same firewall. The failed party must stay vigilant on this point when it accepts aid — not all aid is qualified; aid of the surrogate-liberation kind interrupts cultivation just the same.

Three, the ally's own stake is a healthy anchor. §13's second face established that an intervening party should have a stake of its own, but that this stake must be a structural stake in which its own cultivation position is implicated, not an expansionist interest. The same principle applies to alliance relations: an ally with a real stake of its own in giving aid is more reliable than a purely disinterested one. Stake is not a stain; it is the structural anchor that makes an alliance sustainable. Two kinds of aid must be distinguished here. Non-war aid — shelter, supplies, passage, the preservation of culture — does not pass through Theorem One; any ally that holds to point two may give it. An ally that sends forces on the failed party's behalf is opening a war of its own and must pass Theorem One: the ally's own first face must hold (Gate Four: its own cultivation is being eroded now, not by prediction, and this erosion comes from the same source as the oppression the failed party suffers), and the failed party's plight, as a second face, cannot launch it alone (§13). A purely disinterested ally can be an excellent giver of non-war aid; its sending of forces does not stand under the criterion. What is judged here is the ally's own opening of war. For the failed party, bringing in an ally's forces is an expansion of participants in its own war and is judged separately under §14; for the gray zone of borrowing strength from a 14DD, see below.

Four, the outward direction is not omnipotent. Sometimes there is no ally to be found — the opponent's structure of oppression may be so strong that every surrounding subject has already been engulfed; outside subjects willing to help may be geographically too far away or in crisis themselves; the failed party may be so weak and small that it finds no one willing to support it. In such situations fragmenting-and-dispersing can only go inward, not outward. This is still a qualified response, only at greater cost, over a longer time, and with greater reliance on internal resilience.

There is a yet more desperate gray zone — when the 15DD party is crushed to desperation by 14DD, it may find no ally operating at 15DD and can survive only by borrowing force from another 14DD (the enemy's enemy). This is the gray zone of the SAE criterion in an extreme situation. This treatise cannot pretend that such situations do not exist — human history has repeatedly produced the predicament of "having no choice but to accept the shelter of a 14DD ally."

In such situations, this treatise does not lay down an absolute criterion of "no borrowing force from 14DD" — because that would make the criterion break down in exactly this extreme situation and would instead push the subject toward yu-sui ("better to vanish in glory than borrow force from the wicked" is the 14DD-style purism trap). This treatise's criterion is — if the 15DD party has no choice but to accept a 14DD ally's shelter, it must maintain an extremely clear-headed internal firewall: use the ally's physical shelter, but never internalize its 14DD systemic purpose.

This is an extremely dangerous tightrope walk, one that may turn and bite back at any moment. The 14DD ally's "aid" may at a critical moment become new oppression; its systemic purpose may slowly seep into the 15DD party's organization, narrative, and mode of operation; it may make the 15DD party a tool in its own chain of expansion. Every one of these risks is real.

So is borrowing force from 14DD a qualified response to failure? This treatise gives no simple "yes" or "no." What this treatise gives is — if it is done, the internal firewall must be continuously held; the moment the internal firewall can no longer be held, fragmenting-and-dispersing has already slid into colonization by a new 14DD. Whether it can be held is a judgment within the specific situation, not a universal answer that philosophy can give.

Inward fragmentation and outward connection together make up the complete form of fragmenting-and-dispersing. Both stay alive — inward fragmentation keeps cultivation from being completely overwritten on its native soil; outward connection gives cultivation external support and the possibility of an eventual counter-offensive. Whatever part of fragmenting-and-dispersing takes up arms again — armed resistance under occupation, a counter-offensive alongside allies — is judged by the line drawn in §14: if it continues the original war, it must pass Theorems Two and Three; if it opens a new one, it must pass the three conditions of Theorem One, being able to say how the war will end among them (Theorem Four applies throughout). The ending a failed party can envision is often the dissolution of the threat described in §15: cultivation continues in dispersed form until the oppressor's expansion stops or collapses. The envisioned ending is not conditional on a guarantee of military victory. After entering defeat, the war-party must still update, according to the facts at hand, its conception of how cultivation will continue.

Outward connection, as one direction of fragmenting-and-dispersing, is structurally the extension of Note 9's directionality constraint ("I don't collect") into the post-war / desperate-situation field — alliance relations must protect the failed party's autonomy over the direction of its cultivation, and must not decide the failed party's future on its behalf. This directionality constraint appears during war as "not deciding for the other," after war as §18's surrogate-liberation firewall ("not reconstructing for the defeated party"), and in alliance relations as "not deciding for the failed party." One principle, manifesting in different fields.

The Structural Reality After 14DD Wins

That 15DD has failed means that 14DD has won. What does 14DD do after winning? This is the structural consequence of 15DD's failure, and it is what fragmenting-and-dispersing faces.

The 14DD side that has won, lacking 15DD self-awareness, most readily does the following:

Complete elimination — attempting to erase the failed party as a subject from the root (slaughter, cultural eradication, the purging of historical memory).

Demanding yu-sui — manufacturing in the failed party the cornered sense that "there is no way out but yu-sui." Here yu-sui is the outcome the 14DD winner most wants to see (the enemy eliminating itself, without the 14DD winner having to act, and leaving no ember of resistance).

Deciding on its behalf — deciding everything for the failed party, treating it as an object to be managed.

But all these moves are internally unstable over the long run —

One, the subjectivity of a civilization that has been completely eliminated returns in various forms (the SAE axiom); adversaries through the ages have often faced, a few generations later, the counter-surge of the conquered's descendants.

Two, the 14DD winner interrupted its own cultivation in the course of winning (it needs wartime mobilization, the suppression of internal dissent, and mechanisms of terror to maintain its rule), and this weakens its long-term competitiveness as a civilization.

Three, 14DD winning usually collapses quickly — many of history's great conquest empires declined or broke apart within a few generations. This is not coincidence; it is the manifestation of 14DD victory's internal structural instability.

So fragmenting-and-dispersing in a world that 14DD has won is not merely a passive response — it is a structural forecast of 14DD victory's internal instability. The dispersed seeds of cultivation know that 14DD winners will eventually decline; the work of fragmenting-and-dispersing is to preserve the seeds of cultivation until the 14DD winner declines, so that they can re-emerge in the future.

Time, too, is on the side of fragmenting-and-dispersing. The 14DD winner's maintenance of conquest requires continuous violence and oppression, and the cost of this maintenance rises over time; the cost of preserving dispersed seeds of cultivation falls over time (because the occupier's attention shifts from cultural eradication toward one new crisis after another). Generations later, dispersed cultivation has the chance to re-emerge in new forms, while the 14DD winner's conquest has often already collapsed or been eroded.

Azeroth Examples

The Draenei: The Extreme Form of Continuous Flight

The Draenei's thirteen-thousand-year exile is the longest-scale demonstration of flight — preserving cultivation through continuous migration.

On Argus (the Draenei's native planet), the Burning Legion's conquest was under way. The Draenei prophet Velen recognized the structure of the moment — the Draenei had already split from within, and Kil'jaeden and Archimonde, who accepted the Legion's power, had taken most of the people with them. Continuing resistance on Argus was impossible; surrender meant accepting replacement by 14DD systemic purpose (the Draenei would be remade into weapons of the Legion); yu-sui would terminate cultivation.

Velen's choice was a third path — to walk away. The Naaru (beings of Light) answered his appeal: K'ure took him and the few hundred of his people who were willing to go away from Argus aboard the Genedar, while L'ura stayed behind to hold off their pursuers.

This is an extreme form of flight (sub-situation two) — continuous flight with no safe place to land. The Legion's coverage at cosmic scale meant that a "safe place" could only be a temporary foothold that kept shifting: an entire civilization existing in the form of exile, establishing temporary cultivation at each stop, then leaving again. They wandered the cosmos in exile for thirteen thousand years, moving on each time the Legion caught up with them.

The Draenei's flight combined their own resilience with the Naaru's aid. The Naaru carried them away from Argus and gave them spiritual guidance through the exile; the Draenei themselves relied on Velen's leadership, strict communal discipline, and thirteen thousand years of unbroken persistence. The presence of aid does not remove the exiles' own continuing work of cultivation.

These thirteen thousand years were not thirteen blank millennia — they were the continuous deepening of cultivation in exile. They learned the wisdom of the Naaru, deepened their spiritual traditions, and built the capacity to remain subjects even while in flight.

On the way they settled on Draenor for more than two hundred years and were caught up with again: the orcs, driven by the Legion, slaughtered more than eighty percent of their people. The survivors fled once more, and in the end the Exodar brought them down onto Azuremyst Isle in Azeroth, where they became one of the core races of the Alliance. When they did, it was not by military counter-attack that they returned to the cosmos; it was through thirteen thousand years of accumulated cultivation that they became the spiritual mentors of Azeroth's civilizations, and in the end a key force in the counter-offensive against the Legion.

This is the complete demonstration of not choosing yu-sui — no submission (they refused the Legion's temptation), no yu-sui (they refused to die fighting on Argus), but the choice of the possibility of cultivation's continuation.

The Night Elves: Fragmenting-and-Dispersing Through Local Resistance and Refuge Overseas in Parallel

The Night Elves' response after the burning of Teldrassil is a typical case of fragmenting-and-dispersing. The Draenei show continuous exile at long scale; the Night Elves show another scale — local resistance and refuge overseas in parallel.

The Horde under Sylvanas (which had already slid into operating as 14DD — more specifically, into a form close to the Kingdom of Means) burned the Night Elves' World Tree, Teldrassil — the core of Night Elf civilization's spiritual and material carrier. Great numbers of their people died; their capital, Darnassus, was lost.

The Night Elves did not choose yu-sui. Their response:

Inward fragmentation: Tyrande and Malfurion withdrew with the remaining forces; the survivors went mainly to Stormwind, and some to Azuremyst Isle and Mount Hyjal. Night Elf cultural tradition continued to be preserved in these places of refuge, at various small outposts, in individuals. The worship of Elune, the tradition of nature magic, and the druidic inheritance all continued in dispersed form.

Outward connection: the Alliance was already a multi-race cultivation-network — after losing Teldrassil, the Night Elves received support from other Alliance members (humans, dwarves, gnomes, Draenei, and others). Several members of the Alliance each had a stake of their own (the Sylvanas-era Horde was already striking at the Alliance, not only at the Night Elves), which gave the support a healthy structural anchor.

Waiting as an ember kept overseas: in the later campaigns against Sylvanas, Tyrande became the incarnation of the Night Warrior. This was not yu-sui-style "everyone goes to their death"; it was the Azerothian version of keeping cultivation's ember alive overseas. In the Shadowlands and Dragonflight periods the Night Elves took part again in Azeroth's main affairs — the dispersed seeds of cultivation re-emerging when the moment came.

The Kingdom of Stormwind: Qualified Failure at Short Scale

During the First War, the Kingdom of Stormwind was invaded by the orcish Horde (driven by Gul'dan's gui). King Llane was assassinated by Garona during the siege of Stormwind, and the city fell.

On the surface this was complete failure. But the structure of Stormwind's choices is clear —

Not humiliation: the royal city fell, but the Kingdom of Stormwind did not surrender and accept the orcs' 14DD systemic purpose. The king's assassination itself does not establish a "qualified failure"; the argument of this example rests on the survivors' choice.

Not yu-sui: but neither did everyone die fighting. The anti-yu-sui structure illustrated here lies in the choice that Llane's guardian, Anduin Lothar, made in defeat — to lead the survivors in withdrawal, across the sea to Lordaeron, preserving the people and the possibility of rebuilding.

Lothar's withdrawal was the short-scale version of the flight mode (sub-situation two). In that moment he recognized that Stormwind's concrete form could no longer be maintained, and chose actively to trade time for space.

In Lordaeron, the surviving people of Stormwind joined the other human kingdoms and the dwarves, high elves, gnomes, and other races to form the Alliance of Lordaeron — the typical case of seeking allies outward. The founding of the Alliance let Stormwind, having lost its homeland, go on existing as a cultivation-subject, and then counter-attack successfully in the Second War and reclaim its homeland.

The failed Kingdom of Stormwind rebuilt itself as a subject in Lordaeron, and widened the scope of cultivation's recognition (the Alliance included more races than the original kingdom). This demonstrates that qualified failure can be accomplished even within a short span of time — the key is not the length of time but whether cultivation's continuation was kept through the failure.

Summary of the Failure Posture

Three qualified responses, fragmenting-and-dispersing in two directions, the hard-core position of anti-yu-sui — together these form the complete SAE criterion for when 15DD fails.

All of them rest on the same principle — so long as cultivation itself has not terminated, it is a qualified failure. Qualified failure does not need a glorious posture, does not need a dramatic ending, does not need its descendants to believe that "our civilization died with great dignity." What qualified failure needs is — the seeds of cultivation still there, the possibility of the future still there, the return of subjectivity still in the hands of time.

On the surface this posture looks "not heroic enough." But it is harder than 14DD-style yu-sui heroism — it bears more shame, more ignoble getting-by, more of what "looks inglorious," so that cultivation can truly continue. True hardness is not dying gloriously in defeat; it is continuing to carry the work of cultivation in defeat, until the moment comes again.

This is the complete criterion SAE war theory gives for the ending form of failure.

20. Equilibrium

Equilibrium is the third ending form. It is neither victory nor failure; it is the situation in which neither side can have its "cannot-not" dissolved by war.

The SAE Definition of Equilibrium

Equilibrium: none of the parties — whether two or more — can have the "cannot-not" it held at the opening of war dissolved by continuing the war; that is, fighting on cannot give any party's cultivation back the conditions for recovery.

The key to this definition is — fighting on will not let any party's cultivation recover. The war has reached a military or structural point of balance; neither side's investment of resources, persistence of will, nor strategic position can let one side clearly prevail. Cultivation on both sides is in a state of interruption by the war, and continuing the war will not let any party's cultivation recover.

In this situation, Theorem Three's requirement of being "directed toward ending" becomes — stop the war.
